1 DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

GARDEN - PARTY COSTIJMES

The weather has furnished ingenious won= with a new idea in garden - party costumes, writes a correspondent. She has, lam told, .all her muslin frocks on her hands because it is too cold to wear them; she knows they will be old- fashioned next year—besides. she wants to wear them now, this minute, so she does wear them with her sables thrown over them, and the curi- ous thing is that one has already grown used to the anomaly and rather likes it than not. Dia- phanous frills fluttering from beneath fur/ pele- rines and boas are certainly pleasanter to look upon than cloth or tweed garments would be, and really the one is just as sensible as the other so long as the rain does not come. It would seem, too, as though women had prophesied a . grey summer this year, so great is the prevalence Princess Beatrice of Saxe-Coburg.—Of all the late of black and white in dresses, hats, and parasols, Duke of Saxe-Coberg Gotha's daughters, the Princess and black and white gossamer. Beatrice is the favourite in this country, her eldest SUMMER AFTERNOON GOWN. sister the Crown Princess of Roumania ranking next in bur affections. Princess Beatrice has speut much of A charmingly simple dress for afternoon wear her time, since she came out, with her aunt Princess is shown in our sketCh. It could be carried out Henry of Battenburg, and until Princess Ena's marriage equally well in voile or eolienne; or it would to the King of Spain, was her cousin's greatest friend look particularly well realised in a pretty silver- and constant companion. Princess Beatrice has not grey Shantung. The skirt is gathered at the perhaps the perfect beauty of the Crown Princess of waist, and is prettily arranged with groups of Roumania, but she possesses a delightfully animated two large tucks above the hem. The full bodice face, fascinating manners, and a charming figure. She is almost covered by the large, pointed, cape- is always dressed to perfection, apparently in the like collar, which is finely tucked all over, and simplest, but in reality the most wonderful French gowns. The Duchess Marie of Saxe Coburg has always shown the most excellent taste in dress, a taste appar- ently inherited by the Grand Duchess Cyril of Russia, whose toilettes call down the admiration of all privi- leged beholders. The Square Parasol.—Parasols may be reckoned among the greatest bargains at the July sale, so little demand has there been for them this year. The square shape, finished with a silk fringe, or a fringe of tiny flowers, is much in evidence, but even at the low figure the novelty is marked, it would prove dear to the majority of us. However well the weather may now behave, we can only hope for a few weeks more of- summer, and we should therefore do better to push along with the parasols end en-tout-cas we have, and look about for items likely to be of service when autumn arrives. Furs.—Fewer bargains in peltry are in evidence at the sales than has been the case for several seasons past, and this is probably owing to the fact that we have not long ventured to discard our furs, and that it cannot alas be so very lone before we shall be forced to don them again. Still this is the time of year to buy furs, if buy them we must, and now is the time also when we should get those we have that need to undergo such a process, remodelled and renovated. Fashions in furs are of course constantly changing, but sable, chinchilla, ermine, fox, and mink, are slways safe pur- chases. Stone marten we thonld do wisely to avoid. Last season it became a little too popular to suit exclusive taste, moreover it is distinctly an unbecom- ing fur. There is a whisper from responsible quarters that bear will return to favour when autumn arrives ; it promises therefore to prove an excellent investment , to those who cannot afford the more costly varieties.l And we should do well to remember that a small piece, of good fur is worth all the imitation rubbish at present I on the market. Novelties in Buttons.—The Chinese button of carved; ivory, the carving by the way appearing almost as delicate and intricate as that seen on valuable antiques, bordered with some Cluny lace insertion, carried is the button of the moment. Weird gargoyle faces, up the fronts, and outlining the square-cut open- ing at the neck, the opening being filled in with quaint conventional-looking flowers and greusome dragons, are the usual designs, ind the most favoured a finely-tucked vest of spotted net. Three tiny sips is that of a penny piece. Ouida."—lt is curious how some authors hit upon a Tom-de-plume. That of Mrs. Louise de In Monde. who is known to all the world as " Ouida." is simply child's mispronunciation of her Christian name, Louise. Although she is often supposed to be a Frenchwoman, " Oujda " is a native of Bury St. Edmunds. Her father Was a French refuguee, settled in this country, but her mother, whose maiden name was Sutton, was English. Ouida " was born in the year 1840, and at an early age commenced her many contributions to the fiction of the Victorian era. Her first novel, "Held in Bondage," appeared in 1863, but she achieved her greatest fame with "Under Two Flags" and "Moths," the latter being published in 1880. It is understood that Miss de la Ram4e has made at least £50,000 to .-260,000 by her books, but she appears to be in need of the Civil List penson of £l5O a yerr, recently granted to her for dis- tinguished literary services. She has never married,and for many years has lived in Italy.

IVADGE.

FREE CHURCH CoUNCIL.—SERVICES ON THE CONTINENT.

The Continental Services Committee of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches have con- sidered the question of Free Church services at popular places of summer resort on the Continent, and in past years provision bus been made for English and American visitors. Arrangements are now completed to bold ser- vices in English at the Grand Hetet P,ier, Grindelwald, Switzerland, during the months of August and September. Services will begin each Sunday morning at eleven. The Chaplains appointed are Rev. Dr. J. Monro Gibson, ex-President of the National Council, for August, and Rev. William Cuff, ex-President of the Baptist Union for September.

American Divorce Laws.—The freedom with which marriages are dissolved in the United States is illus- trated by the official report for California. In the twelve months ending June last year, 2133' divorces were obtained or nearly one-seventh of the number of weddings. The principal ground of action was wilful desertion, and other reasons, none of which would be recognised by the Divorce laws of this country, were cruelty, intemperaece neglect to maintain, conviction ior felony, and " infelicity " Women are generally supposed to be so much mere anxious to marry than men, but in California, the wives greatly outnumber the husbands who wish ,to obtain release from the married state, as, in no fewer than 1,545 of the 2.133 eases, the initiatory proceedings were in instituted by the former.

Time was when if it were announced that cer. tam n ladies of fashion were to assemble in the Park riding astride no one would credit the as- sertion. Now women are accepting this mode of riding under certain circumstances as an abso- lute necessity, and many of our best tailors have habits specially for the purpose. In America the skirt of the habit is simply cut like a divided skirt, and completed by a short jacket. Women aro ordering short skirts for walking abroad, which they can wear with their habit bodices, but they would hardly do in England.

THE PRETTIEST YOUNG WOMAN,

A former rector of Holsworthf left money -kJ be given to the most deserving and prettiest young woman most regular in her attendance at church. This year the gift has fallen to Miss Jane Maynard, daughter of a late sexton, and it wa: presented to her at the church porch.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

STRAWBERSTES FONDANT.—A very pretty des- sert can be made, says thO Tribune, with straw- berries, but the firm, ripe berries must be used for these bon-bons. One pint of granulated sugar, half a pint of boiling water, a few drops of vanilla essence, and some firm, ripe berries. Put the sugar into a stewpan, pour over the boiling water, and stir over the fire until dis- solved; then boil, without stirring, until if a little of the mixture is dropped into cold water it can be formed into a soft ball with the fingers Take it off tho fire, add the vanilla, and stir until thick. Then put it on to a bread-board, and knead it like dough until soft. Keep in a cool dry nlace. About two hours before wanted put some ot the tondant into a. bowl, place the bowl on a an of hot water, and mash and stir until the mixture is like cream. Dip each berry into the fondant, and put on oiled per to dry. Only do sufficient at a time for one deBool-t, al thew will soften ia n few hours.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 20, 1907

cariosity. He stepped back from the window„ thought for a moment, then stealthily slid round to the door. It was open; for the night was warm with the oppressive heat of an Australian summer. Without pausing he entered the living- room and looked round him; the sweat of excite- ment, and of fear—for the young matt in the next room looked as strong as Hercules—was standing on his low brow. By the light of the small lamp he saw the box, the ordinary oak chest which stands in the honoured place in nearly every cottage in the Western Counties of England, and he sprang noiselessly at it, lifted the lid, found the spring, and took from the drawer a small tin box. He had scarcely got it in his hand when tle heard the woman's voice again. .3The certificates, Ralph—the certificates!" she gasped. "Yes, mother," responded Ralph. get them." The man in the next room tore .open the lid of the tin case. There was a sealed envelope, and, under it, a few trinkets. He was about to transfer the whole contents of the box to his pockets; but hoe changed his mind and, taking only the sealed packet, he slipped the tin case hack in its place and closed the lid of the old chest. At the very moment of his doing so, he heard the young man's footsteps approaching the door between the two rooms. The thief made for the door, but he had unintentionally closed it behind him. There was no time to open it; the lifting of the heavy latch would have been heard; and he slipped behind the window curtain, and, drawing out a knife, noiselessly opened the blade and waited, his heart beating thickly, his breath coming like that of a baited bull, while his hand closed tightly over the hasp of the knife, and murder looked out from his eyes.. Ralph went to the chest, brushing the curtain on his way and causing the man behind it to lift the knife ready for a thrust. Ralph found the spring, but much less quickly than the man had done, and, taking out the box, returned to the death chamber; again brushing the curtain as he passed. "Here it is, mother," he said. "Open it!" she gasped. He obeyed. Her dim eyes must have seen by the expression of his face that what he had expected to see was not there. "There are only some jewels, trinkets, mother," he faltered.

"No, no ! " she panted. "The certificates, the papers—they were there. I. saw them before I was taken ill; they are there! They must be! Look again. Ralph, your future! You are— you are—the son of-- You will be—! The certificates Ralph ! "

I The death-rattle stopped her utterance. The small tin case dropped from his hand, and he put his arms round her as if he.would hold her back from death. But no one can hold back from Death that which he has claimed. ! Ralph laid her on the pillow and closed her eyes, and sat for some minutes overwhelmed by his bereavement. He had no one else in the world but his mother. And she had gone. After awhile he went into the outer room, and half mechanically put the tin case in its old place. Then he went to the door and looked out on the sultry night, the glittering stars. He was alone in the world, and his loneliness weighed upon him like an intolerable burden. Even if there had been light enough his eyes were too blinded with tears to see a stealthy figure creeping towards the forest—a figure that had in its pocket the sealed packet which had been stolen from the tin case.

The Earl of Lynborough leant back in his easy- ' chair,with his keen eyes—they looked like sparks of fire glowing in an ashen mask, so pallid was the wrinkled face, so bloodless the thin lips— peering from under the long, white, almost flesh- less hands • and Mr. Bolton, the family lawyer, as he looked at his noble client, wondered which phase, which incident of the eventful life was haunting the Earl at that moment. He waited in silence, for he knew his lordship too well to venture to break in upon his meditations. "Yes, I have •made up my mind, Bolton," at last said the thin cold voice. "My experiment has been successful, as you say—though it was your experiment, by the way; you suggested my adopting Veronica. "If so I have cause to congratulate myself, my lord," said Mr. Bolton, suavely. "Miss Veronica---" "Is all that is beautiful, and virtuous, and praiseworthy. No doubt, no doubt! I do- not yield even to you in my admiration for her, and it is well known that you make a fool of yourself over the girl, and think her perfect--" "I accept the word, my lord. Miss Veronica is —perfect. And it is your intention—" "To leave her Wayneford and my private fortune. Talbot will have the . title and the estates, and they should be sufficient for him; • though I doubt whether they will be." He paused significantly. The Earl was not fond of his nephew. "It was enough for me--" "Your lordship and Mr. Talbot Denby are two different persons," remarked the lawyen. "Thanks, Bolton," retorted the Earl with grim sarcasm. "I have no quarrel with my nephew, but I do not think there is much love last between us." "Mr. Talbot is a most estimable young man. He is making a name for himself; is already beginning to loom somewhat largely in the public eye." "He is welcome to loom as largely as he pleases, and I hope the public enjoy the looming. But I am not desirous of discussing Talbot's virtues and manifold talents. We were speaking of Veronica. I *ill ask you to draw up a will which will give her my fortune and Wayneford. On one condition—that she marry a man of rank."

Mr. Bolton pursed his lips. is proud, my lord." • 'I count oh her pride. I cannot rely on it altogether. Bolton, you know there is a bad strain in her blood. My brother married beneath him. Her mother was—" "A most respectable young woman." "I take your word for it. I never saw her." "A worthy young lady, though of inferior rank to your brother. After all, it is not the first time a man has married beneath him." It was a truism ef the most obvious kind; but Its effect upon the Earl was peculiar. His hand dropped from his head, a faint colour rose to his white face, and a gleam—an angry suspicious gleam—shot from the dark eyes, and the black brows above them drew together in a frown; but these signs of emotion passed as quickly as they had come and gave place to the, usual cynical smile as fie said: "Quite so, quite so. But the fact remains tpat the strain is there, and therefore I must guard against it. With my money goes Wayneford Park. It was my mother's. If I care for any place on earth, I care for Wayneford. Veronica will reign there. Ido not propose that some upstart commoner shall reign beside her. -I would rather the money and the Park went to Talbot. After all, as I said, it is a simple condition. We can put it to her. If she should accept it, well and. good; if not--" He made a little gesture with his hand and leant back as if the argument were fleshed. "We may as well ask her now, Bolton." The lawyer shrugged his shoulders. "As well now as at any other time, my lord," he said. The Earl touched the bell beside his chair, a footman entered noiselessly—all noises were strictly barred at Lynne Court—and his lordship bade him ask if Miss Denby would be kind enough to come to the library. After a few minutes the door was opened by the footman to admit the young girl of whom the dwo men had been speaking. As she entered the magnificent library the light from the fire—there were fires at Lynne Court summer and winter, for the Earl 'was always chilly—mingled with the sunlight and illumined her face, so that she looked like a vision of maidenhood at its freshest and sweetest against the dull background of ebony mouldings and inorocco-hound volumes. Descriptions of women's looks are always inadequate and often misleading; and it is difficult to explain wherein lay the charm of Veronica's face—the charm which made itself fAt by all who looked of-Ofer. Her eyes were grey, the Lynborough grey which not seldom Lecama violet; the brows ' above them were dark and straight, like those of the family pertraits ; the mouth was by no means small, but bewitch- ing,with curves which would have indicated more than the usual feminine temlerness especially when she smiled, if it had not been for the pride which lurked at the corners of the mobile lips, ahono in the depths of the wonderful eyes, end revealed itself in the poise of the shapely head. Can one sum up all this with one word— beautiful? "You sent for me Lord Lynborough The Earl rose—Air. Bolton had sprung to his feet as she entered—and the girl stood erect, the taper fingers of 0110 hand resting on the table,

'Miss Veronica

and looked calmly, almost regally, from one to the other. ,

"Yea yes, Veronica," said his lordship. "Will you sit ?" Mr. Bolton brought a chair for her, and she sank into it, gracefully, easefully, with that self- possession of movement which we think, rightly or wrongly, is the exclusive gift of the highly bred, and waited calmly and serenely. "Veronica, I am going to leave you Wayneford and a sum of money—all I'm worth, in fact." The girl did not start, did not utter a word for a time; a faint, a very faint, colour came to her face, and as faint a surprise shone in the beautiful eyes.

"But why---? she said at last, softly- wonderingly. "Why do I leave it to you? Well, I might say because it pleases me; but let me rather say because you please me. Wait "—for she had opened her lips as if to speak again—"wait before you express your thanks, my dear Veronica. There is a condition—there is always a fly in the amber, you know. I think it a simple one, but Mr. Bolton does not agree with me. We shall see which of us you side with. It is that you marry a man of rank." She was silent, but her eyes rested on his. They were expressive, and they displayed curiosity, but no surprise:_. _

"You' think the Condition an absurd one? Good! I will not contradict you. Call it a whim of mine. The fact is here, sound and substantial. Marry a man of rank, include the baronetcy—and Wayneford and my money are yours; that is the condition. It is yours to accept or refuse." She was still perfectly self-possessed, but the colour had come and gone in her face, and she was frowning -slightly. She was silent for a moment or two—to Mr. Bolton, on the strain, it seemed quite a long time—then she said in a low voice, and almost as if she were communing with herself rather than answering the Earl: " Wiyneford--rich. I should be very rich ?* • "So Mr. Bolton says; and he knows," said the Earl in a murmur, as if he were anxious not to break in upon her meditation. • "When I came here," she went on, in a still lower voice, "I did not know what rank, society meant. I had been poor; there was scarcely enough money when my father died to—to—bury him. The Earl's hands closed on the arms of the chair' and his lips twitched. "I did not know, did not understand, nil they meant. I know now. I am a Denby; your niece, Lord Lynborough:--" _ .

I have a carriage, horses of my own, servants to wait upon me, friends of my own class—l used to make a friend of the girl v .}:o waited on us at our. lodgings !—everything I can wish for. But I must give up this, all this, unless--" She paused. The eyes of the two men were fixed upon her—the lawyer's with intense interest, the Earl's with cynical scrutiny. "Unless I consent to this condition? But— but you are not ill, Lord Lynborough, you will not die!" Her voice broke.

"Any moment, I assure you; at least, Dr. Thorne assures me."

Her beautiful eyes grew softer, and her lips quivered. "Oh, I hope not. You—you have been very good to me." "You have been very good to me, my dear," he retorted, with a mile. "You have taken the place of a-- daughter." She gazed at the fire for a moment or two, then she turned her glorious eyes upon him. "Why are you so proud?" she said, in a low voice.

The Earl laughed sardonically. "I'm no prouder than you!" he retorted. "Come, Veronica, you know that I couldn't hold a candle to you in that respect. Oh, oh' no denial! I have watched you! The family fault—it is a fault, isn't it, Bolton ?—runs through you. I've no son of my own." His voice faltered, but only for one instant, the next it was as hard and unemotional as usual. "I don't take a very great interest in my nephew Talbot. I want Wayneford—it was my mother's—to have a proper, suitable master. In fact, my dear Veronica, lam the victim of sentiment. You 'shall have a day to consider." She moved her hand, in a gesture so like the Earl's that the old lawyer was struck by it. "Thank you, I will decide now," she said. "I have a good memory. Mr. Bolton "—she turned to him with a smile-9 know what poverty means. Poverty is a crime. I saw my father die—" Her eyes tilled with tears, her lips quivered. "I know what wealth means. After all, I need never marry--" her voice died away, her eyes grew meditative. "You decide then ? " said the Earl, almost sharply. Line turned her eyes on him and drew her slim, graceful form erect. "I have decided, my lord," she said. "I will abide by the condition. "Good!" exclaimed the Earl, gently. "We will not trouble you any further, Veronica." Mr. Bolton moved towards the door, but the Earl was nearer and opened it for her, and, with a slight inclination of her shapely head, she passed out. And, even to the old and blasé lawyer, something of the sunlight seemed to have passed. out with her. His lordship got back to his chair and sank into it.

"I knew I could count upon her," he said, with the cynical twist of his thin lips. "Draw up the will, Bolton. I'll sign it to-morrow."

THE VERDICT OF THE HEART.

BY CHARLES GARVICE,

Author of "Her Heart's Desire," "In Cupid's Chains," "Just a Girl," "Nance," "The Out- cast of the Family," "A Coronet of Shame," dm

CHAPTER I.

In a rough log hut, which stood just within the shadow of an Australian forest, a woman was 'lying.h lay very peacefully on the bed, with her eyes turned on the window, from which the curtain had been partly drawn so that slit might see for the last time the glittering stars that shone like eves in Heaven; if they were watching and waiting for her departing soul. She was dying very peacefully, though her mind wandered now and again, and a faint smile passed over the white, wan face on which time—the wonderful Engraver !—had traced the lines of a We, and not a long life, of strain and sorrow. Every now and then her lips moved, and a young.man,who was kneeling. beside the bed with . .

• lean:dimmed eyes and tiglitly set lips, caught here and there a word, a phrase, of the 'low and feeble voice.

She was back in the days of her girlhood, in the land of her youth, the land which the exile never forgets, the land to which he returns in moments of exaltation, be they of joy or sorrow; to which his spirit wanders the moment ere it takes its final flight for a better Home even than England. "The primroses—how thick they are this year! And there are daffodils down by the river. He likes me to pick them for him—likes me to put one in his coat. He called me the flower-girl yesterday. It's all wrong; it's all madness, and I know it, I know it! But I can't help it, I can't help loving him. I feel as if I must do just what he says, just what he wants—as if had no will of my own. And I haven't; it's all his, heart, and will, and soul! If he says I must go, I must--" The voice ceased. She turned her eyes to her son; the past faded away and the present came back. She stretched out her hand slowly and laid it on his head.

"Ralph!" she murmured in the wonderful tones which only a mother's love can voice. "I am here, mother," he said, striving to speak steadily and fighting with the sobs that half choked him; for these two loved each other with a surpassing love. "Are you in any pain ? " "No, dear," she replied, "none at all. I just feel tired and as if I wanted to fall asleep. And death is only sleep, Ralph—God's best, last deep sleep. I want to speak to you before I go, Ralph. There's something I want to tell you, something I ought to have told you years ago, as soon as you were able to understand. But it was hard, cruel hard, and I'm a coward, Ralph. I'm not like you, so strong and brave, so ready to face trouble and do the right thing, whatever it costs. I was always a coward, even from the first, and that's -why the trouble came upon me." She sighed heavily, and her hand stole round his strong, sun-burnt neck. "You were always the bravest and best of women, mother," he murmured brokenly. She shook her head. "No, always the weakest," she said, sadly. "I have been dreaming, Ralph, for a little while, been going hack to the past when I was a girl, to the time when—when I was married. I want to tell you about your father. I—l have kept the truth back from you—the whole truth."

"Is it necessary, mother ?" he urged. "Is it necessary that you should harass yourself? My father died before I was born. What does it matter what happened years ago? You and I have been happy together, and the past doesn't concern us any longer." While he had been qpenaing a man, who had come out from the black line pf the forest with a dragging yet stealthy step, slowly approached the hut, and, with a still stealthier step, drew near the window —so near that the light through the curtain, fell upon his remarkably evil countenance. He had the appearance of a particularly all-conditioned tramp. His eyes, set closely together, were bleared, his coarse lips thickened with drink; his hair was short, his chin stubbly, as if they had recently passed under the prison razor. Take him altogether, he was an evil presence to haunt a 'Oman's deathbed. Stealthily he looked rounq the edge of the curtain into the neat, scrupulously clean little room, and his eyes glinted maliciously as they wandered from the face of the woman to the handsome one of the young man who knelt beside her. He looked for a moment—he could only see the young man's face indistinctly—and then cautiously drew behind the curtained portion of the window and listened. • Every word spoken by mother and son was heard by him, though they spoke so quietly. "No, no ! " she said in response to Ralph's remonstrance. "I must tell you. You ought to know; it's my duty, it's owing to you. Ralph, when I was quite _a young

Her voice faded a;vay,-a-look of anxious misery came into her eyes, as if she knew that her mind was going to wander again, and that it might not come back to her. But her efforts were in vain. The present had gone and the past was back again—not the happy past in England, but a miserable and more recent one here in Australia. She was talking to, and mentally cowering before, someone of whom she was afraid.

"Don't, ,oh, don't strike the boy, Jim! He isn't yours, it's true, but he's mine. I've been a true wife to .you; don't be cruel to the boy— to poor little Ralph! I'll give you the money —I can earn some more. There! you can go down to the saloon now. I'll sit up for you. I won't say a word. Don't cry, Ralph, don't cry, dearie ;he won't beat you again. There, there! Come to your mother!" Ralph's face harde.ned, and his teeth clenched. He knew that she was thinking of her second husband. He could scarcely remember.the man; for the brute who had beaten him had deserted his mother when he, Ralph, was a little child. But, though he did not remember his step-father, he hated him with a consuming hatred; and never more than now, when the memory of the vile wretch was darkening the departing moments of his ill-used wife. "Mother!" he cried pleadingly.

His voice recalled her -wandet:itig mind. Siie drew a long breath of relief as if the vision had gone,. but she was still tretnbling. . _ _

"I thought my husband—my second husband, Jim—was back, Ralph, that—that he was beating you as he used to do." "He is not here, mother," said Ralph. "He will not come back; he has been gone for years. He will never trouble you again. Don't think any more about him." "No, no," she assented. "I will put him out of my mind. He was cruel bad to me—and to you. I ought never to have married him—but I was always weak and a coward." Her breath was coming painfully. It was evident that there was not much left in which to reveal the secret which lay like a burden on the fluttering spirit in haste to be gone. . . .

"The p-a,p-ers—the certificates r I've always kept them, kept them secret, kept them from Jim. I've never told him the truth, I've let him think the worst of me; for I knew if he learnt the truth he would want to make money of it, and I promised—! promised I'd never let the world know. I let your father think inc dead. I didn't mind the sacrifice, I wanted to spare him tho shame of being wedded to one so low as me. The certificates, Ralph! They're in the tin box with some of the things he gave me—your "father, Halph. I've hid the box all these years. No one Ints ever seen it but me. It's been hard some- times, for Jim was sharp and cunning; but I succeeded. -Jim never knew—the truth."

Her breath became still more laboured, hcr voice was scarcely audible, and her efforts to leis° it caused the young man an indescribablc anguish. _ _ _ _

"In the box—in the next. room—the box I brought from England—there is a spring under the handle of the drawer—you lift the handle— there is a little knob--"

Her • voice ceased, her eyes closed. Ralph thought she was dead and, forgetting everything in Ills fear, he bent over her and raised her frail form on his broad chest. She was not dead, but she was scarcely breathing, and he reached for Atorne medicine on the table beside the bed and forced some through her lips, and, still oblivious of everything but her condition, ho watched her with breathless agony. . _ _ _ _

The man outside the window- liad heard every word. At the mention of the certificates and the box, his eyes lightened, and his whole face became eager with greed and a kind of wolfish

CHAPTER IL

! Veronica went up the great staircase to her room; rooms, it should be written, for she had been given a suite in the best position, facing ! south and looking over the Italian garden, and: the park and meadows which stretched beyond. They were palatial rooms, furnished in the latest , mode with all the luxury which practically unlimited wealth could supply. This luxury, this wealth, had at first confused and bewildered her. She had been summoned to it from a . "first-floor front" in Camden Town. But there is nothing to which one so, soon gets accustomed as luxury, and Veronica had grown so used to it that, if she had not quite forgotten the old days of semi-gentility—what a hateful combination of words it is !—the splendour of her surroundings had ceased to impress and oppress her. Now, as she looked round at all the evidences of wealth and position, innumerable in detail and large in the gross, she could not help feeling that they were not transient .but perpetual. Wayneford, whieh she had seen and which had recriled to her a rural palace, and enormous wealth were to be hers on one, and the simplest, condition. She was to marry a man of rank. Well, why not? No woman was more free from small vanities than Veronica, but even she could imagine it as possible that she would be able to comply with the condition. In the circle, the county circle, that revolved rotund Lord Lynborough, its acknowledged centre, there were many men of title—sonic of them of historic title; and most of them had expressed an admira- tion for Miss Veronica Denby, the niece of the Earl of Lynborough. It seemed such an easy condition; she had, so to speak, but to chose. At the thought, the straight brows, the Dent, brows, came together, and she made a little moue of distaste. Girls are supposed to be, always thinking of marriage. Veronica thought of it but 'seldom. No man of the many she had met since she came to the Court had made any imPression on her.' They called her cold; and perhaps she was. Most girls are, until their hearts are warmed by the glance of the man for whom they have been waiting since Time began. Then the snow melts and reveals the flower-gemmed plains beneath, smiling in radiance for his delight. After all, she need not marry; it would be grapd to be the mistress of Wayneford, to be rich, to be a kind of Nirgin queen, a modern Elizabeth. She laughed softly and stretched out her sbapely arms. She need no longer dread the future—and how she had dreaded it !—she was Fccuro against the buffets of fortune. Mistress cf Wayneford Her maid knocked and entered. "Your horse is waiting, miss. Shall I send it back? "No, no, Goodwin," replied Veronica. "Get me quickly into my habit—quickly. I am longing for a gallop.' In a very few minutes she was downstairs and mounted, and with a light but firm touch was holding the bigh-snirited mare in hand. As she inissea up the avenue she heard the sound of wheels, and looking round saw the great landau driving up to the entrance. Lord Lynborough was going for his daily drive. Half-way down the avenue she put 'ter mare at the low railing and got on to the grass of the park. and the, groom thundered after her. but

The Man had turned away, and the Earl looked up at her with his faint, impassive smile. "You seem hard to please, Veronica," he said, wearily. "If the fellow's too proud to accept money-" He shrugged his shoulders. "He asked for work, employment, nt,o; money, my lord," she said.

Then he looked up at Veronica with a swift and eloquent glance of gratitude; but Veronica, sitting bolt upright on her horse, gazed straight before her, and made no acknowledgment. Perhaps she thought that there had been quite enough fuss over the man, prompt and plucky though he had proved himself to be.

As she gathered up the reins she saw a spot of red on her left gauntlet. It was blood. For a moment she thought that, after all, the puppy had been hurt—then the truth flashed upon her; she remembered that she had felt one of the mare's fore-feet strike something as the young fellow had darted for the puppy. He had been hurt in body, 'perhaps badly,-in his humane and successful attempt to save the dog, and had been butt in spirit, still more badly, by the Earl's offer of money. She looked at the red spot and then at the tall, graceful figure striding across the moor with the puppy still in his arms and the dog barking in friendly, foolish fashion at his heels. The colour came and went in her face, and her under- lip was caught by the white teeth, as if she longed to ride after him and make some amends. But pride held her back, and with a shrug of her shoulders she rode on.

How should she know„that the shadow of her fate was riding behind her, that the curtain of her life's drama was up, and the tragedy had begun?

REV. F. B. MEYER AND THE FREE CHURCHES. Rev. F. B. Meyer B.A. is now at Northfield in America. Be will return to England next month and after a short trip to Germany will take up special work under the auspices •of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches. The applications for his services have been so numerous that it. has been found quite impossible to satisfy them all at present. He is now booked up far ahead for a great many of the Federations and Free Church Councils of England and Wales. SYLVIA'S DIAMOND RING.

Dv JOHN K. LEYS,

Author of "Mr. Miniver's Marriage," "The Spy's Confession," "An After-thought," "A Maiden's Victory," "In the Grey of the Dawning," etc.

"I am very sorry, Tom—very sorry, but I can't marry you. I'm sure I've told you that before, and I do hope you won't worry me about it any more." An appealing glance from Tom's brown eyes under the brim of his soft wideawake was the only answer. •"I can't think what makes you so persistent. Why should you want to be married at all? I'm sure Mrs. Aikman makes you very comfortable; and I think life would be so much easier, so much more free of distracting thoughts and feelings, •if people were not always thinking of falling in love." "I wish you were more like other girls," Tom said, with a sigh. "What do you mean by that?" asked Sylvia, flushing and drawing herself upright. "Well, if you were not so bright and sweet- looking--" The girl stopped short in the grassy lane and • stamped her foot. The flush in her face deepened. !'istow, you know I hate to be flattered--" "I wasn't flattering you," Tom said, half • sulkily. But she would not be appeased. "I think I can find my way home by myself," she said, stiffly. "4tH right—good-night," said Tom, and he went off whistling. But before he had gone fifty yards he heard the patter of light feet and a rustle as of dove's wings behind him. He turned swiftly, and there was Sylvia, her little hands held out before her, as if in supplication. "Oh, Tom, I am so sorry I vexed you!" He took her hands, but held them only an instant, murmuring something about having • deserved all she had said. - "If you only wouldn't ask me to marry you, Tom," sho said, as she walked by his side; for of course he was walking back with her. "You see, I can't very well leave aunt." "There are heaps of bedrooms at the Grange, • Cylvia--" he began, but she cut him short. "Now, don't, Tom!" she pleaded. He sighed acquiescence. • "And there are heaps of other reasons. I want • to see something of-life. I am tired of Marsh End. It is all very pretty, but I long to see something different. "What would you do if you had your own Way ? " "‘I should go and live in London," she answered, Promptly. "In London ?*1 he exclaimed. If she had said in Pekin he could scarcely have shewn more surprise. "Yes, in London. Why not ?". "But what would you do there ? " "Oh, I should learn typewriting and short- hand—or perhaps--" She paused, as if she scarcely liked to utter her thoughts. "I am glad you don't think of being a nurse," he said, grately. "But some day he will meet some nice girl--" She ran over in her mind the names of her girl friends, decided hastily that none of them were quite good enough for him, and then went slowly up the garden path. A man's voice in the little parlour startled her, and she went in wondering who it could be. A tall figure—it was too dark in the little, low- eeilinged room for her to see his face—rose and bowed to her, and she noticed at the moment that his action was free from the slightest stiffness or awkwardness. He bowed as easily and naturally as another man would say good- morning. She knew at once that the stranger Was not a common man. The stratwer turned on her a look at once admiring and grateful, and somehow the girl understood what it meant, in spite of the growing 'darkness. "We have often spoken of letting some of the rooms in summer," said Sylvia, turning to the stranger "for the house is really larger than me require, we have never done it. But I doubt Whether we could make you comfortable. Lizzie, 'our maid of all work, is a good girl, but an indifferent cook." "I am nothing of an epicure. What I need is rest—perfect rest amid congenial surroundings. I have been working rather hard— Perhaps a little wee bit too hard—and the doctor told me I must recruit." Sylvia would have liked to ask the nature of the work that had been so exhausting, but of course it would never have done to ask questions of so gentlemanly a man. The all-important but somewhat delicate subject of terms was barely touched upon, but the sum voluntarily offered by Mr. Hartford was so liberal that all lingering Iloubt in Miss Marrable's mind as to the wisdom 'of letting her best rooms to a stranger, who had Possibly heard of the silver teapot she kept beneath her bed and intended to run off with it, -finally disappeared.

ran, in tne noneysncrue Dower Welt was cat._.. the porch, and in the little orchard behind the cottage, where the apples were ripening. At first their conversation was confined to the objectEi immediately around them the harvest, tne progress of the fruit, the swarming of a neigh- bour's bees, and so on. But before long their talk began to acquire a more personal tone, and to Sylvia's surprise she found herself saying to this stranger what she would never have dreamt of telling to her nearest relations. Shyly, yet with a simple pride that became her well, she told him of her secret longing to escape from the round of tiresome duties and insipid pleasures that made up her life at Hawthorn Cottage. "Of course, I must stay here for auntie's sake," she said, and did not seem to notice Mr. Hartford's Quick "vesture of dissent. "She is getting old now, and needs some one to look atter her."

"There is another side to that," said Mr. Hartford. with a judicial air. "The Question m;ght be asked, Is it fair or right that youi young life, your most precious years,, should le sacrificed to an elderly relative who, at the best, has not many more years to live? That questior might be asked, and circumstances might arisf under which it would demand solution."

Sylvia was silent. She had often said as mut.. to herself, but as often she had put the thought away from her as something unnatural, unholy, and wholly selfish. Now she was beginning to wonder whether her instinct—so she phrased it —had not been right after all. "I have seen many young lives wrecked on that wretched quicksand, he said, thoughtfully. "False unselfishness by which I mean mistaken or useless unselfishness, has to answer for the ruin of more lives than perhaps any other single cause. Of course, by ruin Ido not merelpmean coarse dissipation or the losing of money, which is really what most people mean when they speak of ruin. To my mind a ship is as truly lost, castaway, if she rots in a waveless, slimy harbour, as she would be if she went down in mid-ocean, with the tempest shrieking in the rigging." Sylvia murmured something that meant assent, though she could scarcely have stated clearly what it was she assented to. She would not have admitted that he believed that a life spent in toilsome duty was lost, yet that was the idea she had endorsed.

"I have long wished to go and live in London, and lead my own life---" "And work out your own salvation, as the saying goes," cried Mr. Hartford, turning his large bold eyes full upon hers. "It seems to me that if you bear the call of duty, you ought to obey it. But that is a matter each one must judge for himself." "Yes, you are right," said the girl, with a sigh. "I don't know why I should trouble you with all these thoughts and fancies," she said after a pause.

"Because instinctively you feel that in me you will find sympathy, a full and free sympathy, a sympathy that only experience could beget. Every human heart yearns to tell its secrets to some one, above all to one who will know and understand, even before an audible word is uttered."

This was exactly the kind of talk that uncon- sciously Sylvia had been hungering to hear. She felt lonely no longer, now that she had a friend who understood and sympathised with her. She looked upon. Mr. Hartford as an exceptionally clever and good-hearted person, and the best bred man she had ever been privileged to know. But when one afternoon he told her that he was an actor, one, too (he thought he might say), who had attained some degree of recognition in his profession, she (metaphorically speaking) went down on her knees and worshipped him. She would listen to his talk by the hour together, while Miss Marrable was superintending house- hold matters, doing Sylvia's neglected work, or peacefully dozing in her high-backed chair. But one person at least did not by any means approve of this state of things. That was Sylvia's second cousin, Thomas Blenkinsop, of the? Grange Farm. To think that a stranger, a me._ come from goodness knew where, should be enjoying the privileges he would have given half his fortune to possess simply because hp could afford to take Miss liarrable's two best rooms at two guineas a week, was maddening! It was almost as though the man were married to her, Tom thought to himself, with an eruption of passionate, blind, devouring jealousy, which no one would have believed him capable of. So hot did he feel about it, that he actually went over to Hawthorn Cottage to remonstrate, and, finding Miss Marrable alone, he poured out to her his grievances, and so convinced her of the foolish- ness of admitting a man of whom they knew nothing to a footing of intimacy that he speedily reduced her to tears.

This, of course, made him penitent at once; but the mischief was done, and when the old lady, in explanation of her unhappiness, repeated Tom's words to her niece, Sylvia 's wrath boiled over. It was well for Tom that she did not see him for a day or two, but when they did meet she told him very plainly that she and her aunt felt quite competent to manage their affairs with- out his interference, and that, unless he could behave like a ilentleman, he had better stay away from Hawthorn Cottage for the future. When Tom heard this his face grew very white. He shut his lips tightly and said nothing, and was seen no more at the cottage.

Days went by and weeks, and the happy autumn fields were bare, and still Mr. Hartford lingered on in his comfortable quarters. The fact was that he was in love with Sylvia, but, having a lively sense of the fatal effect marriage with a beautirul girl who had neither influential friends nor current coin would have on his career, he prudently kept silence, and yet was unable to tear himself away from her. In such situations Nature is generally vic- torious' and this case was no exception to the rule. Hartford's passion triumphed over his prudence, and one golden evening, having over- taken Sylvia on her way home by the riverside, he confessed his love and asked her to marry him.

The low tones of his voice sounded in the ears of the country girl like the most delicious music. She was trembling all over with love and happi- ness. From the very first she had looked on the actor as a kind of superior._ being, and had taken the greatest delight in listening to his stories of the stage,. but she had no idea that his feeling for her was more than liking, and the admiration which every son of Adam feels for a:pretty face. Scarcely able to speak, she allowed him to take her into his arms and press lis lips to hers. And so, without a word from he, the compact was sealed.

Next morning the girl awoke with the feeling that some great thing had happened—that some. thing strange, new, and delightful beyond words, had come into her life. Then she remembered, and hid her blushing face in her pillow. To be always' with Fred—he had told her his name was Ferdinand, but that his intimates generally called him Fred—to belongS to him, to share his home, his career, all that he had--to be led by him into that wider, freer life that she had so often longed to enter—could any lot be happier than hors? Her first meeting with her betrothed that day was a new joy. .indeed, her heart so overflowed vith happiness, that she I quite forgave Tom Blenkinsop for his jealous interference, and would have liked him to be as happy as herself. But of course that was impossible. A few days later Hartford put on the finger of his fiande a ring Which he had had sent down to him from London—a plain hoop of gold Set with a very large diamond. It was so large, and sparked so magnificently, that it made the room and the whole cottage look poor and shabby. Sylvia cried out when she saw it, and there was as much reproach as admiration in her tone. "Oh, Fred!" she exclaimed, "you must send this back at once, and ask them to send you down something cheaper. You told me that we should be quite poor—at first, and perhaps even sometimes be in need. of a little ready money. What a shame, then' to spend so much on a ring for me! As if I Could not believe in your loin: without that! And besides, dear, as Mrs. Payne would say, it is wholly unsuitable to my condi- tion.' What would people say if they saw me

'wearing 30 expensive a ri-ng ? "

The currants now on the market are of a particularly fine quality, and, in view of possible shortage towards the extreme end of the season, housewives are advised to lay in a reserve stock of this most nutritious fruit.

More than once Sylvia timidly renewed her request that he would have the ring exchanged., but he finally put a stop to her pleadings by say- ing curtly that West-End jewellers did not understand that sort of thing, so she held her peace and gave herself up to the enjoyment of this the happiest period of her life. The lovers were together all day, and every day, and it was hard to part. But Hartford had to go in order to teep a professional engagement at Liverpool, and, bravely keeping back her tears, the girl saw her lover drive Off to the station. Oh, how blank did the days seem after his departure! How she counted the time till December, when she might hope to see him again! But this was not to be. At the end of Septem- ber he wrote to her to say that he had joined a company on tour in the provinces, and would not return till the spring. This was a sad blow, and it was a week before the girl got over her dis- appointment, and then a new thought came into her mind. She would go to London and learn.to be aniactress ! She could then play in the same pieces as her husband, and help him to earn money. Instead of being a drag on him, as she had ofter feared she would be, she would be a ' positive help to him!

Of course, her aunt cried a great deal at the prospect of losing her, but Sylvia told herself that it would be wrong to allow her aunt's interests to stand in her way. For did she not belong to Fred? Was it not her duty to fit herself to be his companion and fellow-worker? So she steeled her heart against the impulse to stay behind for Aunt Pen's sake. She would not allow herself to think what the old lady would do without her, any more than she would allow herself to doubt the ultimate success of her plan. In a fortnight she had bade good-bye to Haw- thorn Cottage and all her friends. She did not hide her intentions from them, and she listened to their well-meant warnings, sometimes with amusement, sometimes with indignation.

As for Tom Blenkinsop, he took her hand without a word when he bade her good-bye and for this she thanked him in her heart. She liked him too well to be able to bear reproaches from him; and, as she often told herself, he had no right whatever to criticise her movements. But he was her cousin, and she had known him all her life. She might have guessed too, if she had chosen to think of it, that his heart was not yet healed—very far indeed from being healed. And so, for all these reasons, she was glad that he said nothing; "for which," as she put it to herself, "he might have been sorry afterwards." London was very strangle to the country-bred girl. She had one or two introductions, but they were to people who lived in the suburbs and could not be of much use to her. At first she felt bright, full of hope and confidence, and even happy in spite of her loneliness. She spent ten guineas out of her little store in taking lessons from a theatrical agent, who assured her ,that in all probability he would be able to secure a first-class engagement for her when the period of tuition was at an end.

But this hope was doomed to disappointment. The agent could offer her nothing better than an engagement as bliorus7girl at one of the music- halls at fifteen shillings a week, and this was net at all the kind of thing that Sylvia desired. She left the agent in disgust, and tried to. get a situation for herself on her own account. And now she learnt by the hard teaching of ex- pe:-i ence how difficult—how impossible—it is for a friendless girl to gain an entrance to the stage in London, if she wishes to be an actress and not a dancer. She became so accustomed to disap- pointments that they seemed to her the stuff her life was made of, and yet she felt them just as keenly as she had done two months before. Her spirits became permanently depressed; for days together a smile never visited her face. And yet she kept a brave front. to the foe, and went on fighting.

_ It was not merely the failure of her cherished scheme that was tormenting Sylvia, but the pressure of actual want. Being unused to London, she had spent money at first rather more lavishly than she need have done, and the sum she had brought with her—all she had in the world—was almost expended. But how could she write to her aunt, who had little enough of her own, and ask for help, when she left home in opposition to her wishes? There was only one other to whom she could apply, not for money, indeed, but for help in getting her an engagement. Fred could help her, she firmly believed; but she felt a quite invincible repug- nance to confessing her need to him. She tried to make herself believe that this was because, being her lover, she could not ask a favour from him. It would be belittling herself in his eyes. And then she tried to think that she could not apply to him because he was in Edinburgh, and could be of no use to her there. But at the bottom of her heart there was growing up a fear, so blank, so hideous, that she did not dare to face it.

Her Fred's letters were neither so frequent nor so long as they had been three months before. She knew it, and this was the true reason that she would not appeal to him for help, and yet she would not confess it to herself in words. She would not believe it possible. For Fred was the soul of honour, and it was impossible, therefore, that anything but her own wish, or her own fault, could bring about an estrange- ment between them. To suppose that he would throw her over, or that he wished to do so— that they would never meet again—was to suppose the impossible. She clung to her faith in him as she clung to her other beliefs. And, indeed, in those dark days, the poor girl needed all her faith. She had now come quite to the end of her resources. She had even been obliged to sacrifice her silk evening dress, her, watch, and one or two other things, in order to pay her landlady. How her heart hungered for one of those warm, loving letters that used to come for her from Liverpool in the days that now seemed so long ago! But nothing like them came to satisfy the hunger of her heart and cheer her sad spirit. The weekly letters—they were not daily now! —were filled with theatrical gossip or anecdotes of things the writer had seen or heard, with excuses for not making them longer, but of love there was scarcely a shred. Indeed, the girl felt that she ought to write and offer her fiancé his liberty, but she felt that to do that would break her heart in two. She had not the courage.

For some weeks she had kept her beautiful engagement ring stowed away in a safe corner of her desk. It was too splendid for everyday wear. It would make people stare, she thought, to see such a valuable ring on the finger of a girl seeking a theatrical engagement at twenty or five- and-twenty shillings a week. But she would take it out and kiss it sometimes, not without tears, telling herself that at any rate Fred had once loved her. If he had not loved her far above her deserts, how could he have spent so much to give her pleasure, and to prove to her the esteem in which he held her?

But now the time had come when bile had parted with almost everything' of value that she possessed—except the diamond ring. Never, she told herself, would she part with that. She would rather starve!

But one night her long-suffering landlady brought her a bill which could only be paid by the sale or pledging of the ring. It had to be done, but Sylvia determined that she would only borrow a small sum upon it, as little as she could possibly help, and redeem it at the earliest opportunity. Still, in spite of this thought, it was with a very sad heart that she opened her desk, took out the ring, and, after a long, loving look, put it in her pocket.

Then she went down the street to the hateful place—to the place where women pawned their garments and men their tools, that they might have bread to live another day. And to that she had sunk! To that her pride had brought her! Yet she would not confess that she had done a foolish thing—she would not acknowledge that she was beaten.

With a throbbing heart she passed through the swing door and entered a narrow, pen-like box, which had for its fourth side the pawnbroker's counter.

"I lonly want five pounds, please," she said, almost in a whisper, as she laid down the ring. The man took it up, looked at it through a glass, rubbed it upon something, and quietly laid it down, saying: _

_ "It's only paste—give you half-a-crown." Sylvia felt bewildered. "Are—you—sure ? " she faltered.

"Sure? Yes, miss. There, try it for yourself," and he pushed the ring and a bit of glass towards her. .

And now Tom shewed that misfortune had made him 'a wiser man. He ignored the past entirely.
