sound from a return Issued by the Blackpool Education Committee that Warrington stood seventh in sixteen towns in regard to the number of successes, bat it stood fourth in regard to the average number of dis- tinctions,iso that he thought on every possible ground they had ,reason as a committee to be gratified with the results, and that fact had been duly noted and trans- mitted to the teaching staff of the Secondary School as evidence of the committee's satisfaction with what was being done. The next matter might perhaps be some- what controversial in a slight degree. When he had finished with it, he did not think there would be much room for controversy. The committee as a public body was, of course, naturally subject to public criticism. They were the servants of the public, but having been appointed their servants he thought the confidence of the public, at any rate, ought to be extended 0 them unless there was very good reason for withdrawing it. Criticism which was fair and based upon fact they could not resent. They must welcome it. But criticism which was not fair, which was not based upon fact, or which was based upon distorted statement of fact or upon want 'of knowledge of the complete circumstances of the case, they did not exactly welcome but they were rather inclined to resent it, and be thought justly so. In dealing with the question of the appoint- ment of master of the School of Art, he would briefly refer to the fact that the school had a history—a history, so far as the past was concerned, of which any town might well be proud. He remembered the time when Warrington and Nottingham stood at the head of the Schools of Art of the United Kingdom as the two first schools in the country. He remembered the fact too when in one year no less than twenty-four or twenty-five medals were obtained by students in national competition. For a long period theireputation of the school was maintained up to a very high stan- dard. He regretted to say, and it was within the know- ledge of every gentleman present, that that had not been the case during recent years. He had only to refer to a very few figures to prove what he had stated. He found in 1902-3 there were 231 students in theschool,3l4 examination papers were worked, and they obtained a very fair proportion of passes. The number of pupils in 1906-7 had fallen to 91 and the number submitted for examination to 67. The results were not yet received. But that was not the most important elementin attempt- ing to putbefore the Committee the deplorable condition , —he used the worn advisedly—into which the school had fallen.ln the year 1902-3 they submitted for examination pupils in eighteen subjects; in 1906-7 the number of subjects taken in examinations was only seven—a con- ditioti unprecedented,he thought he might venture to say lin the history of the school. What wasmore important ' still, for the first time, he thought in the history of the school, or at any rate in recent years, not one solitary student was submitted for examination in design—a subject which the•committee would appreciate was of extreme importance in a manufacturing town. He was mentioning that, because he wanted the committee to appreciate how very responsible the School Manage- . ment Committee felt their position to be at the present time. They felt that they had reached a crisis in the history of the school and that if ever it was incumbent upon the committee to select not a man who could just satisfy the minimum requirements of the Board of Education but the very best man which the emoluments they offered would secure for them, now was the time to secure that man. He would remind the Committee that unless the school could be made of substantial use in applying the teaching of art to certain industries of the town, it was of preciou little use to them as a mercantile and manufacturing community. Therefore it was absolutely essential that on that account they should have the very best man possessing the highest qualifications for the teactiing of applied art. The Committee had nothing to conceal. Everything they had done was absolutely of an impersonal and public character. He did not believe in waiting for questions to be asked; he thought it was beat to give the full chapter and verse to start with, because it might happen, and he hoped it would, that if any gentleman had come there with an impression that anything had been done of an unjust character, thatany pertional question had been allowed to enter into the settlement of the matter, or that anything had been done calculated to be prejudicial to the interests of the school, that • after he had heard' what he (Mr. Roberts) had to say, he would have his doubts removed on every one of those points. The committee decided to advertise for a headmaster and fixed the remuneration at £225 with other terms and conditions as set out in the resolution. In response to the advertisement, the Committee received sixty applicatio is. On June 13th a meeting was held, and before a siugle application was considered, it was deemed advisable to set up a standard of qualifi- cation below which no application should be considered. In order to do that Mr. Murray was asked if the Board of Education had any recommendations to make upon the question, and he referred to a regulation which was found in paragraph 46 of the Board's regulations. ,IThese were the regulations in regard to which grants I were given by the Board of Education for successes in art. The paragraph read: "There must be a principal teacher holding the full associateship of the Royal College of Art, an art master's certificate (group 1) or, in excel.tional circumstances, such special qualifications as the Board may recognize." 'The committee decided that they would accept that isuggestion of the Board of Education as the standard ibelow which they would not consider any application. That was to say, that such condidates as did not possess the full associateship of the Royal College of Art and the art master's certificate (group 1) would be left out of the competition. It was quite true that the Board said further "or in exceptional circumstances, such seertial qualification as the Board may recognize." They understood that to mean that in the case of small or comparatively poor districts where it was impossible to pay a man of the standing who would be con- sidered by the Board as the most eligible man for the headmaster's position, a man who possessed certain minor qualifications would be accepted by the Board for an inferior position of that kind. That was one interpretation. The other was that if they as a committee were satisfied to take a man as headmaster who could just satisfy the minimum requirements of the Board of Education in regard to his qualifications, they might have done so. He took it however, that if the Committee, having had the oppor- tunity of obtaining a man or a number of men who fully complied with the highest qualifications required by the Board of Education, and being in a position to pay for such a man, contented themselves with accepts ing a man who could just scrape through with the minimum qualification, the committee would fail in its duty to the institution and to the people of Warrington. They uranimously decided to stick to the maximum required by the Board of Education. That condition left out of the competition 37 candidates, so that 23 remainel Those 23 candidates possessed qualifications very much higher than those of a gentleman whose name he did nor wish to refer to and in regard to whom he did not wish to say a single word in depre- cation of his position or qualifications. But everyone of those 23 held a higher position in art and higher qualifications than the gentleman to whom it was considered an injustice had been done by the action of the Committee. Having got 23 who complied with the conditions. the Committee proceeded to raise the standard higher still and sbv constantly raising it they reduced the number to eight. They thought then they had eight men of eminent qualifications, whom they seold not reduce further without a personal interview. Those eight appeared before the Committee, and in spite of the fact that one controversialist stated that he knew one of the eight who had said that if ho had been selected, knowing the circumstances of the ease be would not have stood in the way of Mr. Pendlebury he (Mr. Roberts) could only tell the Committee that every candidate was asked why he wanted to come and when be could come if he were apppinted,and everyone of them expressed his extreme desire to come mainly because the salary was a very tempting one, and the field of labour was exceedingly important, and it would he a decided advance in his position as an art teacher. He (Mr. Roberts) asked the Committee to take with a considerable amount of salt any suggestion that any one of the eight would have hesitated even for five seconds in accepting the appointment if he had had the oppor- tunity of taking it. The Committee saw the candidates, and so impressed were they with the manner, the personality and the evident ability of Mr. Mawson that no second vote was taken, and he was unanimously and immediately appointed to the position, subject to the approval of the Education Committee. Mr. Mawson's qualifications consisted first of all of the fact that he was an associate of the Royal College of Art and held an art master's certificate in the following groups :-1, 2 and 6. and parts of 3 and 4. Asa master in training he received five years training. He was appointed in the first instance for two years, and so satisfied was the department with his work that the period was renewed for a further two years and later for an additional year, making five years—a most exceptional case. He possessed nine medals and had had experience as head- master at Swindon from 1900-05 and at Shipley from 1905 to date. He was commissioned to visit and report on art education exhibits at the Paris exhibition in 1900 by the Board of Education. He was senior instructor in elementary art at the Royal College of Art for one veer of his training and was also special instructor for design and modelling for the London School Board for One year. The latter qualification was extremely valuable from the point of view he enunciated a few moments ago, of applying the School of Art in its usefulness to certain industries in Warrineton for not only had Mr. Mawson technical training, but work. shop training as well. If they liked, he (Mr. Roberts) would give them on the other side the qualifications of the gentleman whose cause had been espoused so ardently and he (Mr. Roberts)thought ill-advisedlv,in one of the local newspapers, hut he thought it would be unkind in the extreme, after having given them the qualifications Mr. Mawson possessed, to refer to the difference between his qualifications and those of the gentleman who was said to have been done a gross injustice to and whose claims were advocated, not on the ground of the interests of the community and of making the school of usefulness in the trades of the town, but absolutely and entirely on personal grounds. As a public duty hi(Mr. Roberts) had no hesitation in asking them to support the corn mittee in the appointment. He had taken a very great interest. in the School of Art ever since he was a student at it. He knew its needs perhaps as well as most men and he knew also tha very important fact that there was not a single industry in Warrington to-day that was the better at the moment for having the School of Art in their midst. That was not as it should be and the sooner the committee were alive to the fact that they

had the means, the opportunity and suitable buildings and equipment to make the school of real use to the industries of the town, the sooner would they realise the full extent of the duty that devolved upon them in making appointments similar to the one he now sub- mitted for confirmation. He asked them to put aside every other consideration than the one, and to give the committee credit for having veryseriously and carefully come to the decision to recommend Mr. Mawson on the very bost and highest possible grounds. Father WILSON seconded the adoption of the minutes. _ THE DISCUSSION. _

The Rev. J. DAVIES drew attention to the fact that • the School Management Committee as a whole and not a 'special sub-committee, made the selection. Mr. PLINSTOS said he had followed the extremely good statement made by Mr. Roberts, and he thanked hi m personally for having given an explanation which he (Mr. Plinston) thought would be highly satisfactory to every member of the committee. Many difficulties might be removed if they had explanations given when minutes were proposed. A statement had been made that Mi. Pendlebury's qualifications were not sufficiently stated before the Committee and if it, was true that anything was held back which would interfere with the thorough appreciation •of his qualifications, then there was 'unfairness. Mr. Roberts had made it quite clear that the selected candidates were first rate men, and more particularly did that apply to Mr. , Mawson. It was said that a statement was made by the ' Director that Mr. Pendlebury had not qualifications according to the Board of Education requirements and that Mr. Pendlebury wrote to the Board and received a reply that be did possess the necessary qualifications. tie would like that point to be made perfectly clear, so that they could understand that nothing had been done to make it impossible for Mr. Pendlebury to have received due consideration. Mr, ROBERTS said there was not a vestige of truth in the suggestion that anything was kept back from the Committee or that the Director made any such answer as had been mentioned. The whole of the applications were on the table and before one of them was considered the standard of qualification was fixed by the Cora rnittee without regard to anybody and it was fixed on the Board of Education's own regulation 46. All that Mr. Murray had to do was to assist the Committee in eliminating from the GO applicants all the candidates who did not possess qualifications according to the standard fixed by the Committee. -

Mr. PLINSTON --ita-Wif the Committee raised the qualification standard above that stated in the advertisement.

Mr. ROBERTS : We did not 8, any standard in the advertisement. We fixed it after we had got the applications.

CouncillorDr.JosEmi asked whether any regard was made of Air. Pendlebury's position as assistant master. It Was usual to give some consideration to a present master, even though he might occupy a subordinate position. He (Dr. Joseph) had no feeling iu the matter, and be endorsed what Mr. Plinston had said as to Mr. Roberts' able and lucid explanation. Might not the" excep- tional circumstances" mentioned in the regulations. mean the position Mr. Pendlebury held as examiner at the Kensington School of Art under the Board of Education?

Mr. JOLLEY said there might be some difference of opinion on the question of tile art mastership, but there was one point on which he hoped they were all unani- mous, and that was in the desire to convey to the head- master and staff of the Secondary School their warmest acknowledgments of the splendid results of the pupil teachers' examination. They cordially congratulated Mr. Robson and the staff. He (Mr. Jolley) thought. they were beginning to see some of the fruits of the spade work they had been doing for several years past. Councillor WILKINSON said probably most of the members of the committee would remember the position he took up some yearn ago with reference to the School of Art declining. He used every power to get a good assistant aipointed and Mr. Pendlebury was chosen for the post. His .(Councillor Wilkinson's) feelings would be in favour of Mr. Pendlebury being promoted, but he maintained that when a post was advertised and they received applications from various people, they showld consider them with an open mind. Mr. UNSWORTH : I rise to a point of order. There is no amendment before the committee and I don't know why we are discussing the matter. The CH AIRMAN : He is speaking to the minutes. Councillor WILINSON : The experience I have had in this room should be sufficient to show Mr. Unsworth that I don't often stray from the point. (Laughter). Continuing, he said the Committee should appoint the best man they possibly could to lift up the standard of education in tile school to what it was in days gone by. He thought the qualifications of Mr. Pendlebury might be submitted, as when the meeting was over some mysterious individual would perhaps say something had been held back. There had been nothing held back. Mr. Pendlebury was ruled out along with many more because he did not answer to the requirements of the Board of Education.

Alderman MINION congratulated Mr. Roberts on the I very able manner in which he had laid the matter before , the committee. He (Mr. Roberts) was usually a fighter but he had stated the case in an excellent and gentle way, without feeling and with great fairness. It was not necessary to convince many members of the com- mittee of the justice of the decision. What they wanted to allay was the feeling outside that something had been done which was unfair to one or more of the candidates. He thought everyone would-admit that, so far as they were able to judge, the matter had been liis- cussed and settled wholly on its merits. He knew very little of the matter personally. Some people had been at great pains to send him as much information as they could, for which he was very thankful —(laughter)— I because, if there had been an injustice he would have been only too feady to help to right it. Some people i had gone so far as to say that there was a set delibera- tion on the part of the committee to place the standard: so high and the mesh so wide that certain candidates must go through the net. After hearing Mr. 'Roberts' , explanation, he thought there would be no question on , that point. The only interests considered had been those of the students, of the school,and of the borough. Although he (Alderman Tinnion) came to the meeting, with an open mind, he thought it was now fairly well I closed with regard to the matter. There had really ; been nothing said on the other side and the grievance seemed to be imaginary. It ought to be clearly-stated —he had heard it from the Director and others— that I Mr: Pendlebury in his position as assistant master had had given absolute satisfaction. Some people thought t an injustice had been done, but on the other hand be understood the committee fully recognized the very good work which Mr. Pendlebury had done as assistant master. There was no attempt to disparage his work but in the opinion of the committee the headship would be better filled by a man possessing Mr. Mawson's qualifications. Mr. ROBERTS said that in reply to Dr. Joseph, all that could be said on Mr. Pendlebnry's behalf was present to the minds of the committee when the selec- tion was made. If the committee had put aside the very keen sense of duty they all felt, brought about first by the condition of the school and secondly by a keen appreciation of its proper relationship to the town and had been willing to recommend for appoint- ment: a gentleman who could have satisfied the minimum requirements of the Board of Education, they could have recommended Mr. Pendlebury. They, how- ever, felt that their duty was very much mere important than that and that they were bound in the interests of Vie town and of the school to get the very best man available for the money they could offer. They believed they bad got that man. In answer to Councillor Wil- kinson. he was quite willing to hand to the committee for their consideration the comparative qualifications of Mr. Mawson and Mr. Pendlebury, hut he did not, think it was desirable in the interests of the school and certainly not in the interests of Mr. Pendlebury, to publish them.

The CHAIRNIAN said tbe,committee tried to get the best ability the market offered, and that was in the interests of the town, of the school, and of art in Warrington.

- • DEFECTIVE CHILDREN. The communications from the Board of Education as to the presence of several mentally defeltive children at the Latchford R.C. School—which were referred to this committee by the School Management Committee —were considered at a meeting of the School Attend- ance Committee, and, from the information submitted, it appeared that only in two cases were the children unfit for attendance at a public elementary school, and that these would be excluded from the school. Tnstrtic- sions were given for the School Management Committe( to he notified accordingly. ,The minutes of the committee were approved, o, the motion of Councillor ELLisos, seconded by M' PLINSTON.

• SITES AND BUILINNGS COMMITTEE, THE BOLTON SCHOOL. At a meeting held on the 12th July, it was noted that the approval of the Board of Education had been received to the proposed alteration of the western boundary of the site for the Bolted Council School. A letter was submitted from the architects for the school, forwarding an estimate of the extra cost that would be incurred in consequence of the alteration to the western boundary of the site and also of the amend- ment of the drainage scheme. Consideration of the use to be made of the extra strip of land provided b 3 the first mentionel alteration was deferred, and it was resolved that authority be given for the amended drain- age scheme to be oirried out, the additional cost n,A to exceed\C9o Bs. 44.

THE BEAMONT SCHOOL. A statement of accounts having been submitted by the architects 'for the Beamont Council school, it was resolved: (a) That the clerk of works be asked to furnish the committee with a report upon the work ; and (b) that a sub-committee, consisting' of Councillor Lord, Conncillor Wilkinson and Mr. George Walker, be appointed to visit the school prior to its being taken over from the architects and the contractor. The minutes were approved, on the motion of Mr. FAIRCLOUGH, seconded by Mr. WALKER.

WAkRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

THE APPOINTMENT OF ART MASTER.

Mr. Pendiebury's Position.

The monthly meeting of the Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday evening under the presidency of Councillor J. Fulton-Smith. There were also present :—Aldermen Bennett and Tinnion, Coun- cillors Ellison, Dr. Joseph, Sankey, Nield, Evans, Clegg. Ball, Dickinson, Wilkinson, Pemberton, Lord, Barton, Revs. J. Yonge, J. Davies and N. Wilson, Messrs. H. Roberts, A. .1. Jolley, R. T. Fairolough, C. S. Burgess, H. Kingdon, E. Plinston, T. P. Bradslaw, C. E. Parker, C. H. Unsworth, G. Walker, J. SCHOOL KEEPERS' WAGES.

The resolution of the Education .C-o-Miratee at their meeting on the 17th June, 3907, adopted on the. motion (after due notice) of Mr. T. P. BRADSHAM, directing this committee to arrange for the payment of the weekly wages of the school-keepers on Fridays instead of on Mondays as at present, was considered by the Finance Committee, and it was resolved that notice be given to each of the school-keepers that, pursuant to the motion of Mr. T. P. Bradshaw and the resolution of the Education Committee, the last payment of weekly wages under the present system will be made on Monday, the 22nd July, 1907, a payment of three- sevenths of a week's wages being made on Friday, the Nth July, and of a week's wages on each Friday there- after.

Mr. JOLLEY moved and Councillor SANKBY seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. BRADSHAM said that seeing that the school keepers' work commenced on Monday and finished on Sunday, he thought if the wages had to be apportioned they were entitled to four-sevenths before the new arrangement came into force. Mr. PLINSTON said Mr. Jolley might give information which would obviate the necessity for moving an amendment.

Mr. JOLLEY : I have nothing to reply to. Mr. BRADSHAW : If there is no reply I move that the minute be referred back. Mr. PLINSTON seconded with a view to trying to get an explanation.

FaCuer WILSON said that persons outside the com- mittee who were interested in the matter thought there was need for an explanation. He could not understand the resolution himself and should support the amend- ment if no explanation was given. The Rev. JOHN YONGE : May we have this explana- tion before we are asked to vote? Councillor NIELD : I suggest tbat by straining a point the payment might be made five-sevenths. If they are paid on Friday, it should strictly and logically speaking be five-sevenths. Certainly it ought to be four-sevenths.

Mr. JOLLEY said it was somewhat difficult for him to deal with the question in view of the extremely personal nature of the attack made upon him at the last meeting. He could only say that the resolution was the decision of the Finance Committee in accordance with what he told the members at the last meeting. They were bound to comply with the regulations of the Local Government Board as well as with the wishes of the members of the committee. They were not allowed to make payments in advance, which was wh: t the proposer and seconder of the resolution at the last meeting wanted, only they would not say so (Mr. Plinston : Ne, nct). The decision was strictly in accordance with the Education Com- rnittee's resolution. If they were going. to make pay- 'ments they must have a list of the recipients prepared and that list would have to be cheBked by the Borough Treasurer's department. They conid not do all that at the last minute and the course suggested was a perfectly safe way of carrying out the wish of the majority of the committee. If the Committee did not approve of it they could reject it. He had no feeling in the matter.

111 r. PARKER: Is there a day kept in hand? That is the .question. -

'nil) CHAIRMAN: On the face of it, it looks as if a day were kept in hand. . The voting was as follows: FOR THE AMENDMENT. AGAINST. Alderman Bennett Alderman Tinnion Coun. Clegg Coun. Ball „ Wilkinson , Sankey ~Dickinson MI.. Jolley „ Nield „ rairclough Mr. Plinston ~ Burgess , Bradshaw-7 „ Kingdon--7. The CHAIRMAN gave his vote for the decision of the Committee.

GRATIFYING EXAMINATION RESULTS. At a meeting of the School Management Committee an analysis of the result of the Preliminary Examina- tion for the Certificate, 1907, so far as it affects the pupil teachers in attendance at the committee's classes, was submitted. The analysis showed that out bf 21 presented for examination 20 passed, the percentage of successes being 95.2. Four gained three distinctions, two two, and one foul.. Sixteen out of the twenty have been accepted for admission into Training Colleges. In previous years the numbers accepted were :-1904, 18 out of 54 examined; 1905, 5 out of 23; 1906, 15 out of 23. In view of the satisfactory nature of the result it was resolved that the Committee's satisfaction be expressed to the Headmaster of the Secondary School and to the teachers who have been responsible for the UMW

TO-DAY. TO-DAY.

OUR NEW SERIAL. ROMANTIC AND SENSATIONAL STORY BY AN AUTHOR POPULAR IN BOTH HEMISPHERES.

WE have pleasure in announcing that we have arranged for the publication in our columns of a new Story entitled—

"THE VERDICT OF THE HEART." BY CHARLES GARVICE.

AUTHOR OF "HER HEART'S DESIRE," "IN CUPID'S CHAINS," "JUST A GIRL," " NANCE," "THE OUTCAST OF THE FAMILY," "A CORONET OF SHAME," ETC.

Lovers of powerfully-written, sensational fiction should look out for the opening chapters of this new story, which will prove one of the most deeply interesting yet published in this journal.

SOMETHING ABOUT THE STORY.

Mr. Garvice always delineates his hero and heroine with great care. The" woman of the story," in fact, he considers its most important element. In "The VERDICT OF THE HEART," the rble is assumed by Veronica Denby. proud and beautiful, the niece of the aged Earl of Lynborough. Veronica has known poverty— her father's death was hastened by actual want —but she has been adopted by the Earl, and rules his household. • The Earl, indeed, lets her know that he has left her his whole realisable wealth, his nephew, Talbot Denby, succeeding only to the title and entailed estates. The condition is that she will wed only a man of title, and Veronica proudly assents. It requires, therefore, all the author's skill to make it seem probable that she would stoop to love a gamekeeper on her uncle's estate. Ralph Farringdon, however, is no ordinary man. He has recently come from Australia, and gives only vague accounts of his life there. He has good looks unquestionably, and he also shows good breeding. The leader is aware of a mystery concerning his parentage. He has a powerful rival in Talbot Denby, who badly needs the heiress's money to retrieve gambling losses, and Veronica fights hard against what she considers an unworthy predilection. But after Ralph has given undoubted evidence of' nobility of soul, the verdict of the heart pre- vails, and the patrician leaves gladly the splendours of Lynborough Court for an uncertain future without.

The rivalry between the two men, however, leads to exciting events. The secret of Ralph's origin comes to Talbot's notice. It must be suppressed at all costs, and murder is one of the means adopted, the crime being first laid to Ralph's charge. At these incidents, which make up really the most absorbing part of the story, and at the surprising denouement, we can do no more than hint. The reader will find the whole work one of unusual attraction.

"TRH 'YEE DICT OF 1 IL HEART,"

BY CHARLES GARVIOE.

Publisation of this story bags in our columns

The Vote of Censure on his PROT ECTION Majesty's Government with

AND reference to the question of PRIVILEGE. Colonial Preference moved by Mr. LLTTLETON led to a 'somewhat futiie exhibition. Mr. LYTTLEFON argued his case well enough. He showed that the •offar made by the Colonies was sincere and corn- -plete, and against that offer he had no difficulty in placing the refusal of the Government. So far, there was no possibility for quarrel. The futility of the whole proceeiings lay in the fact that no new feature can be introduced by a motion of this kind. and Parliament could only do what by the nature of its present constitution it was expected to. The majority in the present House stands unquestion- ably for Free Trade. It was elected upon a Free Trade basis, and it could not be expected to desert that standard even at the call of the Colonies. That was a position which, however much it was regretted by them, was not misunderstood by the Colonial statesman who attended the recent Con- ference. The representatives of the Government at the outset intimated that even had they been disposed of themselves to listen to the offers made in all sincerity by the Colonies they were debarred from doing so by the very vote of the people who had put them into power. That was an attitude Which they repeated on Monday night, when Mr. LYTTELTON asked the House to assent to a p7oposi- Moo condemnatory of the action of the Govern- Ment in not responding to the overtures made to this country. Probably more interest was taken by politicians in the position of Mr. BALFOUR than that of the Government itself. The Government is Free Trade pure and simple. Mr. BALFOUR is Pre- ferential but not Protectionist. The difference is easy to him; it is not so plain to everyone. Mr. ; BALFOBR'S position has been complicated, too, by the claims of various sections of the Unionist 11 Party. His relative, Lord HUGH CECIL, has argued that because Mr. BALFOUR declared that he is not Protectionist in the sense that Mr. CHAPLIN understands the term he is necessarily a :Free Trader. That is obviouly a misconception. i :But its repetition gave to Mr. BALFOUR'S speech on the Lyttelton motion an interest which Was anterior to the subjects immediately ender debate. In a sense all this is unfortunate. The object of the Lyttleton motion is not quite Obvious. The Unionists, however strong their ease, could not hope to convince their opponents, and the vote given by the House of Commons could only emphasise the weakness in Parliament of those who wish to respond to the advances of the Colonies. The public at large cannot be expected always to appreciate the subtle influences which govern Parliamentary strategy, and the Opposition and the Government cannot complairn if people outside are inclined to regard the occupation of 'Parliamentary time, already over-crowded, With a motion which could lead to no definite results as something like Waste. Parliament in accordance with the general rule, should rise some time in Angust. 'lf the prorogation does not then take place, great inconvenience willies, caused to business men both in the House and outside it. But its programme of legislation, as Lord CAmpeanowN and Lord LANsDoWNE showed on Monday in the House of "Lords, is in a condition which gives little hope that the Houses may be able to rise at the appointed date. The House of Commons on Monday devoted itself for a short time to the vestion of its own honour. Mr. LEA, a Liberal Member, had, in a letter to the newspapers, brought charges against the Leader of his own Party of having conferred honours in recognition of subscriptions to the Party war-chest, and had suggested that Members Who received payment of their election expenses horn that war-chest had been compelled by the 'Party Whips to vote against their convictions. L 3rd B,oBERT CECIL wanted the House to declare this letter a breach of its privileges. He was not successful in his object, because, although the PRIME MINISTER and Mr. BALFOuR each declared their regret that unfounded charges of the kind had been made, both agreed that it would 'alot be consistent with the dignity of be House to take measures against the bon. Member. The "country will be glad to read the declarations of the L3aders on either side that there is no truth iO allegations of dishonourable proceedings of this kind. The House of Commons has always been • ilalous of its integrity, and there is no reason to buppose that these who are members of it at the 'Present moment care less for its reputation.

Probably nowhere, with the MR. W. H. LEVER'S exception of Port Sunlight VICIORY. and Liverpool, was greater satisfaction felt thGn in tirrington at the result of Mr. W. H. Lever's 'ibel action against the "Daily Mail" and Mandated papers. Mr. Lever's career as a soap- .ll.aker and commercial man, commenced in vie arrington in 'lBB5 and the town is jealous 13'. its connection with such a captain of

===== 0002601_19070720_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
4," 7.:•••

industry and sl generous a philanthropist. Toe "Daily M-ils" crusade against the short- lived but famous soap combine will not readily be forgotten. It was conducted with an unscrupulousness and a personal vindictiveness against Mr. Lever, which have never been equalled in any Eqglish newspaper campaign. The combine was shown in the worst light possible, public pre- judice was fostered, and the bona fides and even common honesty of the promoters were questioned. The methods of the Yellow Press, represented in England by the "Daily Mail,"were fully employed in this campaign of untruthfulness and misrepre- sentation. Mr. Lever has performed a greater public service than even the "Daily Mail " pro- fessed to be engaged upon, by bringing to book the perpetrators of the slanders which so lowered the tone of the English press. The damages of £50,000 which the offending syndicate has agreed to pay, though they constitute a record, are by no means excessive. Still, they are sufficient to teach a salutary lesson to the type of journalists who have made sensation their aim, and thrown both truth and principle to the winds.

Our references to the TEE ART MASTER, appointment of an art master have not been with• out public advantage, inasmuch as they drew from the Deputy-Chairman of the School Management Committee (Mr. H. Roberts) a lengthy resume of the reasons which prompted the Committee to prefer a candidate from Shipley in Yorkshire to the local assistant master whose ability is unques- tioned. We do not doubt that the Committee's sole desire was to obtain the best man possible in the interests of the town, but we venture to ques- tion their wisdom in drawing a hard and fast rule which debarred the application of Mr. Pendlebury. If they had wished to make it impossible for the local man to have the ghost of a chance they could hardly have found a simpler or more effective method. Apparently the crux of the matter lies in the degree A.R.C.A. (associate of the Royal College of Art) which Mr. Pandlebury does not possess. Whether it is very material is, we think an open question. Mr. Roberts at the close of his ' remarks, said that" all that could be said on Mr. Pendlebury's behalf was present to the minds of the Committee when the selection was made." Seeing that the applicant himself was given no opportunity of stating his own ease the remark , loses much of its weight and significance as an argu- rnent. Were the Committee aware of the fact that Mr. Pendlehury was for some years an examiner of the Royal College of Art prior to the institution of the A.R.C.A.degree? Mr.Pendlebury's qualifications are, in many quarters, considered excellent, and at any rate they were sufficient for the Board of Education. The matter is, of course, now at an end, and we have every confidence in the gentle- man the Committee have been at such pains to select. All we desired was that the local applicant should not be denied promotion without sufficient reason, and we still think that it would have been fairer, and certainly more courteous, if Mr. Pendle- bury had been allowed to appear before the Com- mittee in common with eight oltside applicants in order to advance his qualifications for the appointment.

Most of the Warringtonians LETCHWORTH, THE who visited Letchworth, the GARDEN CITY. Garden Ci'y, were simply charmed with what they saw last Saturday. Flowers and shrubs in the streets, open spaces in front of the houses—no possibilities of slums, as there is no crowding of houses together, 12 •houses to the acre being the limit —delightfnl walks right across the fields. Most of the hedges have been removed and alto- gether there is a delightful air of freeeom about the place which makes one feel the misery of being so "cribbed cabined, and confined "as happens to be the case in most of our manufacturing towns. The Garden City is 6 miles in extent. It is patent to all who visited it that we should not in War- ington be able to match such a schgme, except by way of small samples, for several reasons. Toe land at Letchworth cost £4O per acre. In War- rington the cost is over £2OO per acre. Rites at Letchworth are 2s. 81. in the 2, Warrington suburbs 4s. in the 2. It will thus be seen, apart from any other consideration, that the promoters of the Warrington scheme start with a heavy handicap as compared with the promoters of the Letchworth scheme. The Letchworth Garden City scheme has certainly demonstrated on' thing already-and that a very important one i.e:, that under such ideal conditions and beautiful surround- ings, the death rate is reduced by one half. It is evident that those who advocate a reform of our Land Liws have, if only from a health point of view, a strong case in support of their ideas.

There is cause for gratifica- GRATIFYING tion in the results of the EXAMINATION recent preliminary examina- RESULTS. tion " for the certificate" so

far as they affect the pupil teachers in attendance at the Warrington Eiuca- tion Committee's clasees, and it is only fitting that the fact should be recognized. Out of 21 presented for examination 20 passed—a record which is ex3eedingly creditable. Moreover it should be noted that Warrington stood seventh in sixteen towns in regard to the number of successes, while the distinctions obtained are particu- larly noteworthy, Warrington taking fourth place in this respect. The Education Committee has placed on record its sense of satisfaction with the results and the Headmaster and staff of the Secondary School have received the special thanks which their work in preparing ,the candidates undoubtedly merited. A great deal has been spent upon this institution, and the ratepayer is entitled to look for a high standard of efficiency. Toe returns just recorded are an evidence that the educational machine is now in thorough working order, and that substantial pr-)gress is being made in the work of training the young for the teaching profession.

How many people who ENGINE DRIVERS. travel to and from town realise the responsibility of the engine-driver? Every mother is fond of exclaiming that her child, or children, are a great responsibility. But the engine-driver is sometimes solely responsible for the lives of hundreds of people who are seated in the various compartments of the train entrusted to his charge. In some of the magazines we have bewildering pictures of rows upon rows of signals which the driver must observe when controlling a locomotive from one paint to another. It is only when a scene such as occurred recently between Brussels and Antwerp is described in the Press that pe-,pla appreciate the fact that not only the guard, who hands luggage in and out of the carriage, but the engineer iu charge of the train, on its safe arrival at one's destination deserves a' small gratuity by way of an expression of thanks. Human nature is the same in all races and amongst all classes. Action is controlled by intellect, and intelligence is the starlight of the brain. If that light becomes erratic in the skull. of a duke or a dustman people say, "Poor fellow," and are glad that there are mad-houses. But an engine-driver is not allowed to go mad. The other day, in Belgium,he defied that rule. Regardless of signals, he sent his engine at full speed through one station after another, driving his train to destruction, until the stoker, perceiving the driver was a maniac, struggled desperately with him and finally over- powered his master. How the train escaped being run into was a miracle, but that stoker deserved a V.C.

Of the many devices for THE THEORY OF producing the psydaological PUNS. mystery known as a laugh, punning is one which lasts under a peculiar, and in our opinion at) unfor- tunate, ban. We would be the fist t ) agree that a bad pun is an abomination, but a really neat double meaning, many specimens of which are to he found in the splendid comic poems of TJM Hood, ought really to be considered above the lowest form of wit. Take, for example, the lines : Ben Battle was a soldier bold, And used to war's alarm • But a cannon-ball took off his legs,

So he laid down his arms. Here is a neat, successful pan to which objection would in our opinion, be mere affectation. Bat

===== 0002601_19070720_art0050.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====


===== 0002601_19070720_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, July 20, 1907

( when the same writer says that Deutenant Lars "half-pay would not half pay his debts," the result is not so satisfactory. The phrase sounds harshly in the ear, the pun is too obvious, and there is no real humour in it. In the former case the figure of Ben Battle laying down his arms and becoming sn armless and legless trunk is distinctly funny. Tom Hood's own rule, to which all puns should conform, is that two meanings should be conveyed and it is for reader or hearer to select which mean- ing he will accept, or to accept both. Each meaning must, therefore, be admissable. If when a jar of jam is mentioned someone says it jars on him, no object whatever ia served by the juxta- position of the two words of the same sound but diffarent meaning. If a jam-jar had been knocked over and its contents emptied into the punster's flap he might have some excuse for saying "This jar's on me." Here the two possible meanings of the phrase would both be true. In brief, a good pun should really have a double meaning. If one of the alternative interpretations is without , meaning the pun is a wicked failure. In the words of Johnson, we may perhaps truthfully say: "He who would perpetrate a pun Of that kind) would pick a pocket."

Junior Conservative Drive The third evening drive of the season in connection with the Warrington Junior Conservative Association was held on Tuesday, when some 600 visited Dares- bury and Stratton. The weather was ideal. The conveyances left the club shortly after half-past six, and Daresbury was reached in good time. After a halt' for refreshments the visitors Proceeded on their journey along Hatton-lane on to Stratton for lighting up, and Warrington by way of Stockton Heath was safely reached at 10.30. 21bs FIG & LEMON 6d.—ASIILEY'S Sankey st.—Advt. Treat to Boys from Cottage Homes. The annual treat to 100 boys from the Padgate Cottage Homes took place on Saturday on the ground of the Cheshire Lines Engineers Cricket Club.—The boys left Padgate by the 210 train for Warrington and on arrival marched in procession (headed by the Band) to the field. Unfortanately it was raining at the time, but even this did riot damp the spirits of the boys. The rain cleared at 3.30 when the boys entered into some good old English sports, and at 5 o'clock went across the Lawn to the Albion Hotel, into a large and commod;ous room, kindly lent by Mr W. Jenks (owing to the field being wet) to partake of meat pies, tarts, Eccles cakes and ginger beer. When their appetites had been satisfied they returned to the field and received their 'presents and prizes kindly distributed by Miss Smith. After the band had given several selections, they formed in procession to the Central Station and left by the 7.10 train for Padgate. The Committee desire to take this opportunity of thanking all friends who kindly helped them to provide the treat.

Trip to Windermere. The annual picnic in connection with the Warring- ton and District Off-Licence Holders' Association tosk place on Thursday, to the Lake district. Beautiful weather favoured the outing which proved a complete success. The party, numbering about 60, 'included the President (Hr. W. Throssell) and the Secretary (Mr. Robert Smith), and left Bank Quay station at 7.11 in saloons. Windermere was reached at 10.30 and the visitors proceeded to the Royal Hotel, Bovrness, where an excellent dinner was served. Afterwards , a two hours' drive throng beautiful scenery Ito Troutbeck and other places of interest was enjoyed. The delightful mountain views presented a succession of impressive pictures which will never be forgotten. On returning to Bowness tea was served at the [loyal Hotel. There was a little speech-making,the President briefly referring to the work of the Association, and , urging the members to rally round the committee. , Captain A. F. G. Potter voiced the thanks of the party to the committee for the excellent arrangements. The group was photographed on the lawn behind the hotel. Windermere was left at 5.50 and Warrington was reached at 10 o'clock after an eminently successful excursion.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

A fine large" bull " was perpetrated on Monday night by Alderman John Grime who in addressing the Oldham Athletic Football Club asked them "not to start kicking at fleabites." o "Teas provided. Customers' own vitals cooked." So runs a notice appearing in a picture of a rustic dwelling which "Punch" reproduces as having been "sketched from nature." . .

* * * Though the weather was almost everywhere brilliant, a curious phenomenorn was observed at the "Daily Mail "offices on Wednesday. There was a complete absence of Sunlight and everything appeared to be shrouded in gloom. o o In our correspondence column an ex-Warrington housewife testifies to the boons and blessings which accrue from a smokeless atmosphere. t3he concludes : " We feel cleaner in house, body, and mind since we left the town and its smoke behind."

* * Mr. Aylward will take with him in his retirement to the rural delights of Sussex, the good wishes of a great many Warrington people other than art students. He is one of those individuals blessed with a kindly nature and the power of inspiring other folk's regard. * * * Right in the middle of the Peace Congress England invents d new kind of bullet that will liberate the human soul at a greater distance than ever. Eternal peace is a beautiful thought—but that new bullet will be regarded as a great comfort all the same.— " London Opinion." * * * " That ether nations should beat us now and then at the games we have stolen f :•om them will do us harm only if it makes us lose faith in that illusion of our superiority to all the rest of the world, for to that illusion our success is greatly due."— Herace Hutchinson, in the "Daily Graphic."

- - Parr's Bank Limited, we are pleased to note, con- tinues to enjoy remarkable prosperity. At the ball- yearly meeting to be heli in Warrington on Thursday next a balance sheet to the end of June last will be presented, showing net profits for the half-year amounting to nearly a quarter of a million pounds. A half-year's dividend at the rate of 19 per ceat. per annum is declared.

* * * Mr. A. H. Rathbone's resignation of the offices of secretary and manager of the Warrington Co-operative Society has just taken effect and his many friends will wish him long life and the best cf health to enjoy : his well-earned retirement. During his connection with the Society extending over forty-two years he has worked untiringly and it is undoubtedly due in a great measure to his prudent and economical management 1 that the Society stands in such a sound position. Many of our readers will hear with deep regret of . the death of Mr. Thomas Mcfarlaue, of Ottawa, Canada who, it will be remembered, addressed a meeting arranged by the Warrington Chamber of Commerce at the time when the fiscal question was the great topic of the moment. This sturdy Scotsman spoke very temperately on the subject of preferential trade and those, including ourselves, who were opposed to his policy, could not but admire the sincerity of his advocacy. * * Warrington firemen have had a busy time this week, for they had not long ceased their labours at Messrs. N-aylor's when they were summoned to another outbreak in Buttermarket-street. Fortunately in this case the fire did not assume .very alarming propor- tions, which was a great relief to occupiers of adjoin- ing property who had begun to remove their goods into the street, in anticipation of the flames spreading. Great damage was done to the pawnbroker's shop in which the outbreak originated, and many pledged articles were destroyed. Perhaps the first example of namesakes in different spheres that would spring to the lips of most readers is the case of the two Winston Churchills. Oddly enough (says "Cassell's Saturday Journal "), they are both the sons of American mothers, and both of them have written books of stirring quality. So much so that most people might be excused for not being able to say right off the reel which of the Winstons wrote " Savrola "and which " Richard Carvel." The con- fusion became all the greater when the young English- man who had a price put on his head by the Boers kivaded his rival's own domain, and went off for a lecturing tour of the States. * Some of the residents of Cockliedge, we are told, think an unwarranted aspersion has been cast on that neighbourhood by our note in this column on the novel joke of the in;lividual who chalked " Beautiful Warrington " on a wall there. We did not mean anything unkind, and we are quite ready to believe that many of the inhabitants of this district are not by any means insensible to the Beautiful Warrington ideal. In Platt-street in particular there is a fine display of window boxes full of choice blooms ! and we are only too pleased to commend the efforts of those who are doing something to make this locality brighter. * All Warrington was attracted to Howley on Wednesday night by the great fire at Messrs. R. A Naylor's timber works. A memorable spectacle was presented, the flames shooting to a great height, while showers of sparks were sent forth. Disastrous ! as the fire was, it would have been much worse had a strong wind been blowing. As it was, the flames were confined to one block of buildings which was almost demolished. The firemen's work has earned praise on all hands, for they grappled with the flames splendidly and got them under control in a com- paratively short time. As a prominent member of the firm pithily expressed it, "it was a tip-top save." * * A good story is being told at the expense of a well k,nown local gentleman who is an enthusiastic collector of curios and articles which bea r the stamp of antiquity. Recently the requirements of a modern business necessitated the demolition of prorrty of a great age and during the course of (the work one of the labourers tluanced upon an old English coin, which he promptly appropriated. A little while after the aforesaid gentleman visited the premises and beamed with delight when the find was shown to him. With the eagerness of a born collector he immediately became the possessor of the curio in exchange for cm-rent coin. Determined not to miss any other interesting discoveries he apin interviewed the workmen and was delighted to find that each of them had unearthed something —an old photograph, or coins—and again there were mutual exchanges which left both parties satisfied. Instructing the men to retain anything else which was found, the gentleman left exulting„ but his joy would probably have been more short-lived had he heard a burly navvy remark to a pal " If th' chump is going to give a tanner each for coins I will find some, even if I have to hide 'em myself."

Crasfield's Fire Brigade. - -

The members of Mess. Crosfield's fire brigade took part in the annual tournament in connection the with North Western District of the National Fire Brigades Union which was held at Great Crosby on Saturday. There was a large attendance of firemen from all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire and a very successful series of competitions was gone through. Messrs. Crosfield's brigade appeared in all the drills and secured the first prize in the hydrant and the third in the hose-cart drill. Superintendent Gallon was in charge. The proceeds Of the tournament were in aid of the widows' and Orphans' Fund of the Union and realised £5O —a very creditable result considering the unfavourable Nvpather. A deputation, consieting of the Rev. Harry V. Pigot, chairman of the Infirmary Board of sfanagement, Dr. E. A. Fox, and Mr. C. Jackson flohnes was requested at the meeting of the Board, held on the sth of July, to wait upon Mr. and Mrs. Henry Greenall, for the pnrpose of presenting them with the following vote of thanks, passed on that date :—" That the most cordial thanks of the Board of Management of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary be conveyed to Mr. F. nd Mrs. Henry Greenall for their kindness in allowing the use of their grounds, at Hill CHIN, for the grand bazaar and fete, held Jane 19th to 22nd, 1907. in aid of the fund for the extension of this hospital." _ _

The deputation visited Hill Clide yesterday and was received most heartily by Mrs. Henry Greenall, who expressed her regret at the unavoidable absence of her husband. The Rev. H. V. Pigot, being engaged in parochial work, the resolution was read to- -Mrs. Greenall by Dr. Fox, the proposer of the vote of thanks and presented to her on vellum. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FLOWER SERVICES 4T RUNCORN.

The annual flower services at the Primitive Methodist Church, Greenwav-road, were held on Sunday. when there were good congregations. The preacher, morning and evening, was Mr. John Wright, of Glasbury, who delivered stirring sermons. In the afternoon there was a special service, over which Mr. J. Scrag g presided. Mr. Wright gave an appropriate address. Miss A. M. Midge gave a recitation, and special musi&was rendered by the choir and Sunday school children. The collec- tions showed considerable advance over last year. SENSATIONAL AFFAIR AT FRODSHAM.

A sensation was caused in the village of Kingsley, near Frodsharn, on Friday evening, when it became known that during a quarrel which is alleged to have taken place between a cattle dealer and his wife both their throats had been cut. Abcnt half-past four the husband and wife were seen to rush from their house holding their heads back, and it was noticed that their throats were cut and that blood was streaming over their clothes. A constable who happened to be passiug. rendered them what assistance he could, and summoned Drs. Barton and Selby. The man's condition was so serious that his removal to the Chester Infirmary was ordered. It is alleged that the man cut his wife's throat with a pocket-lmife, an& afterwards rushed upstairs and cut his own thrtiat with a razor. THE WIDNES TRAGEDY.

At Liverpool Assizes on Wednesday Michael Sullivan (62), labourer, was indicted for the manslaughter of Thomas Hosker, at Widnes, on tbe sth inst. Mr. Riley and Mr. Cockshutt appeared for the prosecution, and Mr. Foley defended.—The case for the prosecution was that about eleven o'clock on the sth inst. there was 1 a row in the lodging-house of which Hosker was the deputy. Husker was a man of about forty-five years of age. Prisoner was blaming someone for stealing is meat, and made a charge against Hosker, whom he struck over the head with a clog. The medical evidence would show that Hosker suffered from heart disease and ' was in a bad state of health. Waat the jury had to inquire into however, was whether death was accelerated by the blow given by prisoner.- -John Joyce, rag-gatherer, gave an account of the row in the lodging-house, saying that prisoner alleged that some " two-legged cat" had stolen his meat. When Hosker came into the room the prisoner charged him and pushed him into a bedroom, where witness heard a fall. Hosker died about half an hour after- wards, prior to which he said, " I've been struck on the head with a clog."—Dr. Hutchinson, Widnes, deposed that Hosker had diseased lungs and tho valves of his heart were diseased. He died from heart disease, and his death might have been accelerated by a blow, a struggle, or any excitement.— The evidence of arrest having been given, prisoner was called into the witness-box to give evidence on his own behalf. He had said something about his meat having •been stolen. He had had some drink. He denied having hit Hosker on the head with his clog. It was true they had a struggle in the bedroom, and they both fell against the bed. WAness had two teeth knocked out.—Cross-examined : Hosker was only doing his daty in seeing prisoner go to his room and get to bed. Prisoner could nc°-, understand why anyone should say that he had struck Hosker with a clog.—Mr. Foley submitted that the prosecution had failed to make out their case. If the jury had any doubt as to what prisoner had done or what was its effect he was entitled to acquittal. The jury found prisoner not guilty, and he was discharged.

WIDNES SPORTS.

The fortieth sports meeting of the Widnes Amateur Athletic Club was held on the Football ground on Friday evening and Saturday afternoon. The evening meeting was well attended but rain spoiled the gate on Saturday. Still, probably 5,000 people witnessed the events on the two days and the sports were certainly a success. A peculiar incident happened on Friday evening. In the five miles cycle race, confined to members of the Widnes Cycle Club, W. Hulton, S. Cash. S. Hatton, R. Allen and G. Glover turned out to com- pete for the club's silver challenge cup and gold medal. For some reason the men had apparently pre-determined not to race, and the spectators were treated to a unique display of "crawling.' The riders proceeded round and round the track at a snail's pace, chatting and exchadging handkerchief's. So slow was their progress that on more than one occasion they found 'difficulty in maintaining their equilibrium. The hand enteredironically into the spirit of the affair and played" Sing me to sleep." "Lead, Kindly Light," and similar lively (!i tunes. In sixteen minutes ths riders covered the tremendous distance of three miles, and the judges then declared the race off. The spectors hooted the riders as they left the track. It is said that the fact that no lap prize was offered led the cyclists to insult the spectators and officials in this remarkable way. It was a most regrettable incident. 012 Saturday some particularly good riding was witnessed at the halt- mile race. The long distance events saw keen com- petition and honest trying, while all agreed that the quarter-mile was a finely-coutested race. The chiaf events of the sports was the one mile flat race for the .N.C.A.A. championship, which was won rather easily by J. Roberts, the popular Sefton Harrier. Only two runners competed. The" scratch hundred" resulted in a popular win for Hargreaves, of Southport, after a fine race. Details

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON,

MR. J. A. SEDDON'S EPIGRAM FOR THE SOCIALIST.

Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., speaking at Stockport on Sunday night upon Socialism, said that Socialists were often charged with believing in free love. It was dis- honourable of their opponents to hurl such a charge against them. As Socialists they believed there could be no home where there was no chastity, love and honour.—A man might be a Socialist and not be a Christian, but it was absolutely impossible for a man to, be a Christian and not a Socialist.

ORANGEMEN'S PARADE AT HAYDOCK.

On Sunday afternoon the annual church parade o! the Ashton-in-Makerfield district of the Loyal Orange Institution was held at St. Mark's Church. Between 200, and 300 members from Ashton. Earlestown, and Hay- dock assembled at the Colliery Schools and marched in procession through the principal streets to the church. The service was conducted by the curate-in-charge, the Rev. T. S. Stoney, who preached from the text," Be ye clean that bear the vessels of the Lord." - 'MESSRS. McCORQUODALE'S.

On Saturday about 32 members of the staff of the above firm left Newton at about one o'clock for a drive, by coach to Hale, through Warrington, Moorr., Runcorn, and crossing the Mersey and canal by the Transporter Bridge to Widnes. Tea was provided at the Child of Hale Hotel,and the party afterwards spent a very pleasant time in playing at bowls and visiting the places of interest in the district, the quaint old church coming in for a fair amount of attention. The route on the return journey was through Farnwortho. Newton being reached at about ten p.m. The arrange- ments were carried out by Mr. Hindley, end gave - satisfaction to all.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS POLICEMAN ASSAULTED. John Hardman, a young man living in Tontine-street, pleaded guilty at St. Helens on Monday to charges of drunkenness and assaulting the police, and William Price, a companion, admitted being ' drunk. Police.. constable Anders said he found the men engaged in a stand-up fight in Barrow-street at eleven o'clock on Saturday night. They fought their way into a private house and disturbed the occupants while at suppe Hardman butted him in the face and kicked him.— Hardman was fined 15s. and colts and Price 10s. and costs.

GAS EXPLOSION AT ST. HELENS.

Considerable alarm was caused in the neighbourhood of Rigby-street, St. Helens, on Saturday evening by an evplosion which took place at the house of Mr. John Fairhurst, 52, Rigby-street. The explosion or—mrred when Mrs. Fairhurst and her son Lambert., aged twenty- one, were in the bedroom, and it wrecked the ceilings of the parlour, and kitchen, blew the windows into the street, and cracked the lobby wall, besides blowing the partition wall out of position. Mrs. Fdirhurst went into the bedroom about 7.45 p.m., and noticed a strong smell of gos. She called to her son, who struck a, couple of matches, with the inevitable result that the gas exploded.

ST. HELENS LABOURER'S SUPPOSED SUMIDE. On Saturday night the Present police recovered the body of William Taylor, a widocver, forty-nine yeaas of age. of St. tfelens-road, Prescot, from a pond in Eccleston Nursery. Dee. .sed was a labour, employed at Sutton Heath Colliery, St. Helens. About half-past six on Saturday night hi left home for the purpose of going to Eccleston. Shortly afterwards a young boy saw the man in the middle of the pond with his head above the water. He ran and informed some men, and Police Consts.hle Guest came on the scene and recovered the body, but life was extinct..

FOOTBALLER'S ESCAPADE AT ST. HELENS. David Traynor, the well-known St. Helens and Swin- ton Rugby footballer, Appeared before the St. Helens magistrates on Tuesday as the result of a drunken escapade the previous night.—The Chief Constable called John Oldham, a youth, of Claughton-street, who said he was going along George-street about seven p.m. on Monday when Traynor and four other men were standing by the side of the street. Traynor ran out and pitched witness and another boy off the handcart. Traynor threw one of his mates on to the cart and then ran it up and down the road. The cart was smashed. Then Traynor and his mates went along to the Hippodrome, and he picked up a basket of oranges belonging to a girl named Rose Everall. He also struck the girl,and used very bad language. Traynor pleaded that he had no intention to do any harm. He denied that he assaulted the girl. Mr. Ellerington said prisoner had been there ten times before. He was a footballer, and at certain times he believed that the streets belonged to him and that he could do as he liked. He seemed to think that it was very clever to be knocking people about. Traynor was fined £l. is. or twentv-eight days for the theft, £1 4s. or twenty-eight days for the assault, and 9s. 6d. or seven days for damaging the handcart.

PAfIISR COUNCIL. The statutory meeting of the above Council was held in the Wesleyan School on Monday. Dr. T. A. ,Murray, J.P. presided and the following members were present: Messrs. Woodward, Withenshaw, Houghton, Routledge, and the Clerk, Mr. E. Foster. The minutes 'of the last meeting were read and passed. Seed were re-elected managers for the Wesleyan School on tho proposition of Mr. Withinshaw, seconded by , Mr. J. Woodward.

The Clerk read the following letter from the County Council removing the disqualification attached to Mr. James Woodward re his charge for repairing the fountain !cc.

"At meeting of the County Council of Lancaster held at the County Hall, Preston: on Thurs- day the second dav of May, 1907, present, County, Alderman W. Scott Barrett (chairman), &c., resolved that upon the recommendation of the Parliamentary Committee, and in accordance with the power given to the County Council by Section 46 (3) of the Local Government Act, 1894 'the dis- qualification attaching to Mr. James Woodward, Et mem- ber of the Penketh Parish Council, by reason of his undertaking the keeping in repair of the Jubilee Fountain, etc, in the parish, be removed, as in the opinion of the Council the removal of such disqualifica- tion will be beneficial to the parish." (2) The abandonment- of the Penketh Station by the Registrar of Births and Deaths • and (3) The unprotected state of the pit in Station-road. The Chairman promised that the pit should have the attention of the Council.

--With regard to the first question, the Council resolved into Committee, and on the return of the Press repro- tentatives, no answer was given on this matter. The Clerk asked the reporters to call the attention of the parishioners to sea that their names were on the Voters' List, and to notify him of any omission. CONGRATULATING TIfE CHAIRMAN. He did so by moving the following resolution :—" That the, members of this Council beg to offer their congratulations to Dr. Murray, J.P., on his election as Chairman of the Rural District Council and his elevation to the Magis- terial Bench and that the Clerk record the Council's satisfaction upon the honour conferred on the Chairman" Mr. Withinshaw seconded and the mbtion was sup- ported by all the members present. Dr. Murray thanked the Council for their kind remarks and said he considered the honour was to the parish, and he hoped to do justice to both the parisl. and himself. The meeting then closed.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

In the matter of the weather Warrington have been very unlucky this year and their inability to complete matches on account of rain makes their task of lifting the championship one of great difficulty. On Saturday they should have met St. Helens Recs and had every reason to look for victory when rain prevented any play, and instead of a possible two they only counted one point.

The league tables to date are : FIRST DIVISION. Club P. W. L. D. Pts. St. Helens Warrington Earlestown Prescot St. Helens Recs Stanley Newton

SECOND DIVISION. Earlestown Prescot Warrington Newton St. Helens Recs. lO 3 6 1 7 St. Helens Stanley

The second eleven just managed to secure the victory in their encounter with St. Helens Recs second. Owing to rain the start was considerably delayed and when play commenced St. Helens had first knock. With nine wickets down they had 90 runs on the board and as there was only an hour left to play the closure was applied. St. Helens had, however, reckoned without their hosts and Bainbridge and Dolan commenced to bang the bowling about. Playing against time both took great risks but the end justified the means. „Bainbridge scored 27, and after several batsmen had failed Thewlis joined Dolan.

Of late Thewlis has given evidence of great hitting Dowers and in record time on Saturday ran up 13. Warrington when the last over was bowled required two to win. Dolan was facing the bowler and bad several balls trotted un from which it was impossible to score. The last ball was delivered before his oppor- tunity came and stepping out he drove it for two amidst the cheers of the spectators. Going in second wicket he was not out for 25 and played a really splendid innings.

--- Castle Rubber made but a poor show when opposing Fletcher's in the second round of the Works Competi- tion and were all out for 24. Fletcher's proceeded to knock off the runs required to win in 10 minutes, and when their 50 minute innings was concluded were 101 for G wickets.

Swimming.

Warrington are evidently too good for Everton, and on Wednesday succeeded in defeating them for the second time this season. The match was in the second round for the Northern Counties Championship and was decided at Margaret-street Baths, Liverpool, on Wed- nesday night before a fairly large company. In the first portion Warrington defended the deep end. Play was at once taken into Everton quarters, and C. Ashton and Pemberton put on points for the visitors. The shooting of the homesters was very poor, and several times the ball was sent wide of the posts. Half-time—Warrington 2 goals, Everton nil. On changing ends the Everton team were again unable to make headway, while C. Ashton added two more goals to the Warrington total. Result :—Warriogton 4 goals. Everton nil.

This is the first time that Warrineton have qualified for the semi-final of any cup competition, and it is to be hoped that their victory will encourage them to aspire to creater honours in the semi-final when War- rington meet Bolton.

St. Helens are due at Warrington on Thurgday even- ing next, this being a Liverpool and District match. In the away match in .Tune last the game resulted in a draw of four goals each, hnt with the advantage of the bath the local team should about win.

On the same night the second round of the Works Squadron Competition will be swum off, the competing teams being Co-operative Stores v. Walker end Sons, -and Pearson and Knowles v. Post Office. These two events should produce some good swimming.

===== 0002601_19070720_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Religious Education.

Rev. J. R. Jones Addresses Sunday School

Teachers.

The quarterly meeting of the Warrington and District Sunday School Teachers Association, which took the form of a garden party was held in the Vicarage grounds at Padgate on &ant-lay. After tea a service was held in the Church, when an address was given by the Rev. J. R. Jones, vicar of Sankey, on the text "Train up a child in the way he should go." He (the preather) honestly believed that every Sunday 'school teacher should be a full communicant of his church, as the office was the highest, grandest, and noblest anyone could take in hand. In this age, when there was so much wrangling in the political world as to whether a child should be taught his duty towards 'God and his neighbour, or whether it was better to turn him out into the world and let him find out these things for himself ; when the political atmosphere was charged with a spirit of secularism, when the forces of unbelief were marshalling themselves together to assault the founda- tions of Christianity, it was as important as ever that -the Christian Church, should make no mistake, but declare her intention to carry out the injunction of their divine Lord and Master, that the child should be brought to Him. It was idle to deny that the Christian Church was threatened. Never since England had been a nation had such a threat been held over the heads of the inhabitants as was held over it now. Therefore it came to pass that men and women who valued the "faith once delivered to the saints" were presenting a bold front, and were working hard and sincerely for their belief that, whatever others might say, they maintained that without God nothing would be really •strong and truly holy. It was a mistake to say that a nation could be strong without God; such a proposi- tion could not be established. They must start right if they hoped to end right, and whatever otbers might say that above all things the fear of the Lord was the beginning of all true progress.

LErrga RETURNED AFTER 31 YEARS.

Thirty-one years ago Captain Owen, of the Crown Inn, Carnarvon, was absent on a voyage to Mexico. His wife wrote him a letter addressed to Mexico, affixing the then ordinary shilling postage. The captain did not receive the letter. This week Captain Owen's scan at Carnarvon was surprised to receive, through the dead-letter office, the letter his mother had written to her husband before her son's birth.
