WARRINGTON

Volunteer Band at Manchester. The Warrington Volunteer Band, under the con- ductorship of Bandmaster Richardson, played selec- tions of music in Cheetham Park on Sunday.

Ambulance Successes. At Northwich Parochial Fête on Saturday, the War- rington corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade won the fifth prize. Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons team gained the eighth prize. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANOASHIRB.—Advt. Memorial to Poynton icar. The parishioners to Poynton have decided to erect a suitable memorial to the late vicar, the Rev. T. Bridge, M.A., a native of Warrington who received his early education at the Blue Coat School. The proposal is to spend about £2OO in providing a lych- gate for the main entrance to the churchyard. Local Athletic Successes. At Altrincham Rose Society sports on Saturday, J. Leather, Sankey Harriers, won the 100 yards race off the ten yards mark, and was also third in the 200 yards race. L. Green, Sankey Harriers, carried off the third prize in the quarter mile. At Wrexham sports, J. Spencer, of Warrington, won the 120 yards handicap for competitors who had never won a first prize, and he also won the first prize in the quarter- mile race from the 5 yards mark. Bay's Foolhardiness. _

the dangers attending the practice of children of "hanging on" to vehicles was emphasised on Monday. About 4.30 a horse and lurry belonging to Mr. E. Isherwood, and laden with wire, were going along Crossley-street, when a boy named Gilbert Allen, aged five years, of 12, Back Crossley-street, commenced to swing on the back of the lurry. His right foot was trapped between the spring and the wheel, and before it could be released, the lurry, containing iron, had to be unloaded. The lad was taken to the Infirmary for treatment.

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LA.NCASHERE.—Advt.

Local Athlete Injured

C. Berry, the lea-ding Warrington cyclist, met with a serious accident on Saturday whilst riding in the mile championship race of the N.C.U. at the Man- chester Wheelers' Sports. He won his heat in 2min. 23sec.—the best time of all the heats—but in the final was badly thrown. The final was ridden so slowly that the pistol was fired, declaring the race void. Then the riders made a spurt in the last lap, and three, including Berry, of them came off. The Warrington man fractuwd his collar bone and was taken to the Manchester it nfirmary, the officers of the St. John Ambulance Corps having rendered first aid. Wycliffe P.S.A.

McEachern presided over the Wycliffe P.S.A. meeting on Sunday and related some of his experience, when engaged on the sea. Mr. Dance, of Newton Heath, was the speaker and dealt with " The Ideal Character, What a man is, not what he says." Icicles, if water were pure, would present a beautiful appear- ance, but if the water was muddy, they could not do so. So in their lives they must be pure in heart, and life would be beautiful. A man would then be good at home as well as abroad. Mr. Joseph Garner and the Secretary also took part in the meeting. Mr. Frank Hickman presided at the organ, and played in a skilful manner.

CHINA. TEA SETS.—Special show of latest art patterns. See windows, J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge- street, Warrington.—Advt. Fatal Fall. Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at the Police Station, on Tuesday morning, on the body of Mary Ann Welch, aged 84 years, 35, Dixon-street, who died on Saturday as the result of a fall. Hannah Dalton, deceased's daughter stated that about 11.45 on Friday last Mrs. Welch left the kitchen to go to her bedroom. Shortly afterwards witness beard a noise, and on going upstairs, found deceased lying on the bedroom floor. Witness asked her what was the matter, and she said she was standing on a chair to close the window, when she slipped and fell. Dr. Fox was called in, and he found her to be suffering from a fractured thigh. She died at 4.30 on Saturday after- noon. A verdict of "accidental death" was returned. Druids' Picnic. The members of the "Lord Elliot" Lodge of Druids on Saturday held a picnic to Bollington. The party numbered about 30 and was conveyed by 'bus to the Stamford Arms. Here a game of bowls was played between the teams selected by Bros. Hardman and Hall, Mr Hardmans team winning easily by 54 points. Later an excellent tea was partaken of, after which the Firth Glee Party, consisting of Messrs. Carter, Kenwright, Holloway, and the Bros. Brb.-Ns,entertained the members with glees and songs, which were highly appreciated, other songs being given by Bros. Hall and Blackwood. The thanks of the members were given to Bros. Hall and Palmer for their services in arranging the outing. Warrington was again reached at 10.30.

Robbed the Horses. At the Borough Police Court, on Monday, James Seed, Mill-lane, was charged with stealing a quantity of provender, the property of his empltyer, Edward llsherwood, contractor; and George Peplow, King- street, was charged with receiving the same.—Mr. T. S. Steele, who prosecuted, said that the prisoner Seed was in charge of the Mill-lane stables, and it was his duty to see that the horses under his care were properly fed. On visiting the stables on Friday last, Mr. Istierwood noticed that the horses were looking in poor gondition. It was a very serious case, as prisoner was not only robbing his employer, but also was acting in a cruel manner to the horses.—The magistrates fined Seed £4 and costs or two months, and Peplow 40s. and costs or one month. LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STR,EET.—Adtv. Suicide. An inquest was held at the Police Station on Tues- day morning on the body of John Wilkins (44), wood turner, who resided at 14, Sutton-street, and who committed suicide last week.—Elizabeth Wilkins, wife of the eceased, said that the previous Thursday night her husband came home the worse for drink. Witness left him in the kitchen while she went for her child at the front. On her return twenty minutes later she missed him. She searched for him and eventually found him hanging by the neck from a. rope attached to a beam in an outhouse in the back- yard. She informed the police, and artificial respira- tion was tried, but without success. Deceasel had been drinking heavily for some time ; and he often said he would put an end to himself.—The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide during temporaryl insanity."

Attack on a Wife. John Hargreaves, Russell-street, was charged at the Borough Police Court on NTonday with an aggravated assault on his wife Loniga. Mrs. Hargraves stated on Saturday night prisoner awoke in a mad fit and stabbed her in the chest with a knife (produced). She ran out of the house, screaming, and met P.C. Lewis. The officer said the woman was bleeding very much from her breast. He went home with her, and Hargreaves said, " It's a pity I did not finish her. You get out of this house or I will serve you the same." Prisoner rushed at Lewis, who closed with him, and knocked him down. P.C. Fish took the woman to the Infirmary, where her wound was stitched. Mrs. Hargreaves said there had been no quarrel, but the prisoner came home the wcirse for drink. Hargreaves, however, alleged that he found his wife drunk in bed. Prisoner was sentenced to a month's imprisonment, as his wife did not press the case. GARDEN TENTS.—Tennis,Prognet, and other out- door sports.—.l. S. GARLAND, 51, Brie-street, Warrington.—Advt.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 20, 1907.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

- Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-pi/one may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington. and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each. week.

lam away on holiday. The weather is fine, the company excellent. Ido not wish to be disturbed. For a few days at any rate, politics and all that politics stand for are as dead letters. Even Mr. C. E. Parker's letters do not tempt me. When I return to the hum-drum of town life, I shall doubtless become again permeated with its ideals (?) For the moment, I'have given myself up to the rest cure.

Where I am there are no notice boards warning trespassers that they will be prosecuted. The air is free and so is the land —or a lot of it at any rate. I will reply to Mr. Parker's letter on my return. In the meantime, it would be helpful to know what he means by every man getting the fruits of his labour, the whole fruits and nothing but the fruits. As I write, men are carting hay from the fields ; some are busy stacking it ; ot,i ers are carrying refreshments. I am set wondering —to whom does the hay belong ? To the gatherers, or the carters, or the stackers ? To the landlord or the farmer ? If they all get the fruits of their labour how much do you think will the refreshment carriers get? The sun is shining and the dogs are barking for my company. I must away. lam just going to drink your health.

JOHN WORKAAN.

PUBLIC HEALTH-REFORMS WANTED.

An address delivered by Councillor Stott at Bridge Foot last Sunday evening on the Medical Officer's report is deserving of notice in these columns, and should be of special interest to the working classes seeing that they are the greatest sufferers from the unhealthy conditions which exist in Warrington. The figures quoted from the report regarding the death rate and other matters were of such a character as to impress any man or woman who gives any considera- tion to questions of sanitation and health. Councillor Stott pointed out that although Warrington is a com- paratively small town, and surrounded by open country, the death rate was considerably higher than the average for the 76 great towns. He also showed the striking difference in the death rate between Whitecross Ward with 12.8 per thousand, and St. John's Ward with a death rate of 209. The report states that St. John's Ward has a population of nearly 11,000. It contains some very bad slums, and yards and alleys innumerable It was also pointed out to the audience that St. John's Ward has no open space or recreation ground, and the speaker suppested that it would be an advantage from a health standpoint to demolish some of the slums and provide open spaces with the land. The figures quoted by the speaker regarding the infantile death rate were nothing less than tragic. The rate for England and Wales is 133 per thousand births, whereas Warrington has a death rate of 157 per thousand. In connection with the infantile death rate we have the same alarm- ing contrasts between the Wards as there is in the general death rate. For instance, Whitecross Ward had last year an infantile death rate of 110 per thousand births whereas St. John's Ward had an infantile death rate of 214 per thousand. If we are to be guided by scientists and experts in hygiene, who state that under healthy conditions the death rate of infants should not exceed 5 or 6 per cent. of the births then the remark of Councillor Stott that three out of every four of the children born are being murdered is no exaggeration. One important fact in connection with the high infantile death rate is that the conditions which are responsible for, the death of so many have a detrimental effect on those who survive, which is seen in later years. Another distressing feature which was referred to by the speaker, was the fact that last year there were 110 deaths from Phthisis in Warrington. It has to be recognised that persons suffering from this disease are a danger to the public, the more so if as often happens, the poverty of the last stage of their lives prevents their being isolated and properly cared for.

It is becoming recognised that the State has a duty to perform both for the amelioration of the patients themselves, and for the protection of the more fortunate remainder who are free.

Councillor Stott pointed out to his audience that the powers of the Health Committee in dealing with cases of Phthisis were very limited. He suggested that the Small-pox Hospital at Sankey ought to be used a Sanatorium where cases of Phthisis could be isolated and treated in accordance with the best medical and scientific knowledge. This being a question which so vitally affects every individual It does appear that for our own protection the Warrington Town Council ought to take every precaution to prevent the spread of this terrible disease. Many other matters of interest to the workers were referred to by the speaker, but which we can only briefly mention, such as the smoke nuisance, the miles of unpaved streets, adulteration of food. In the time of Edward the Ist, an Act was pissed to prevent adulteration of food. Any butcher, baker, or grocer found tampering with the quality of his goods was dragged through the streets and pilloried, a method of punishment which appears to have been very affective in stopping adulteration. In connection with School Hygiene we were told that out of 1,009 children examined by the late Medical Officer, not less than 921 were found to be more or less defective, ,105 of them being mentally deficient. Referring to the comments in the report regarding the declining Mlth rate, the speaker said that he was more concerned about saving those who are born. - The reforms advocated by Councillor Stott were demolition of all slum property ; paving of streets and back passages before houses are occupied ; provision of more open spaces and playing fields, better and cheaper bathing facilities, registration of births within 48 hours; compulsory. notification of phithisis cases,l systematic inspection of school children ; municipal milk supply; security of employment at wages suffi- cient to live in decency and comfort.

We must confess it is rather a large programme, but every reform is a much need one. We commend them to the readers of this column for their earnest con- sideration,

THE FABIAN SOCIETY.

The Secretary of the Fabian Society writes I venture to ask you to let me inform 'such of your readers as are secretaries of debating or mutual improvement societies, or any similar organisations connected withieligious bodies or political parties, that the Fabian Society is making arrangements to supply lectures for next autumn and winter on the wide range of subjects covered by the terms Socialism and Social Reform. Besides the well- known speakers of our Society, who always have more applications for their services than they can meet, we have numbers of younger men and women not yet famous who are capable speakers and well acquainted with the subjects they undertake. In the London area such lecturers can always be supplied. Elsewhere we will do our best to deal satisfactorily with applications made to us. Applicants for such lectures should give full particulars both of the dates and times of the lectures and of the size and class of the audience ; and if at any distance from London they should state whether the travelling expenses of the lecturer can be paid.

Volunteer Parade.

An interesting parade in connection witn the Ist. V.B,P.W.V. South Lancashire Regiment was held on Saturday. To the number of 550 the men assembled at the Drill Hall at 4.30 and proceeded by way of Sankey-street and Stockton Heath to a field at Appleton kindly lent by Mr. Lambert. Here they exercised in battalion drill, &c. Later refreshments were served on the field,and the men returned to the Drill Hall, which was reached at 9 nine o'clock, by way of Latchford and Bridge-street. On the outward journey the Mounted Infantry company acted as right guard, but rejoined the battalion at the cross roads just south of the Appleton Rifle Range. The Cyclist company acted as left flank guard and pushed on a section to secure Appleton Thorn ; and when in possession of Appleton Thorn strong patrols were sent forward by the Cyclist company to reconnoitre Arley* and Great Budworth. On parade were Lieutenant Colonel Edelsten (commanding officer), Major J. D. Fairclough, Captains Roberts (acting as adjutant), Crosfield, Smith, Owen and Layton, Lieutenants Monks, Picton, Lepage, Roberts, E. Fairclough, and Captain and Quarter- master Potter, V.D.

MR. C. B. AYLWARD'S RESIGNATION,

Presentations from School of Art Students.

Mr. C. B. Aylward, who recently resigned the head- mastership of the Warrington School of Art and leaves this town for Sussex, was on Wednesday evening the recipient of a handsome gift from past and present students of the School of Art. The presentation took place at the school in the presence of a large gathering, Councillor Fulton Smith (chairman of the Education Committee) presiding. Mr. W. H. Sherburn wrote apologising for the absence of his son Mr, Harry Sher- burn and paying a tribute to Mr. A3lward's devotion to art.

The CHAIRMAN said they had come to a period which was usually marked as eventful in the history of one's life. They did their daily duty and they did it with a love of duty and with that satisfaction which always , followed faithful work. It came as a pleasure when ; they found that, unknown to themselves, their faithful- ness in duty had been appreciated and looked up to by others. It was in that spirit that they gathered there that night. Mr. Aylward who had been in the town for a good number of years was now about to sever his connection with it, and occasion was being taken to let him see that he had been admired and respected. (Applause.) Mr. MILLARD, modelling master at the Manchester School of Art, and a former pupil of Mr. Aylward's, said he had been asked by the Committee to make the presentation. He was certain that if the presentation had been more generally known all the • old boys" would have been present, for he knew that they appre- ciated the efforts of Mr. Aylward. A great many people tried to regard the School of Art at the present time as not being kept up to the standard of a much earlier period. They forgot entirely that Ithe conditions of education in art were abso- lutely opposite to what they were in the old days. If they went through any School of Art in the kingdom and compared the honours boards they would find that where at one period the §tudents obtained twenty or thirty medals in one year, only a solitary one, two or three were won now. Committee members and 'some townspeople had an idea that the falling off was due to certain conditions which could easily be altered and they tried their best to throw it on to somebody's shoulders. There were many other causes besides certain ones that people might think answered for the present type of success, and not simply in that school but in schools all over the country the conditions and the results had altered. There were other circum- stances which were most disheartening to art teachers. Schools of Art were supposed to be built for the benefit of the industries and the general education in art of the nation. In the first place a great many industrial people fought shy of the schools. The masters of works did not tell their apprentices and workmen to go to a School of Art to learn a certain , part of art which might be of benefit to them in their I workshops. In France apprentices were compelled to I spend part of their" time' in technical or art schools. ,It rested more upon the business people of Warrington than upon the school as as to whether the institution was to be of value to the town or not. It was for the inhabitants to take advantage of the facilities provided, but he was afraid it was almost a disposition of the Englishman to lose opportunities that very often foreigners would accept with the greatest gusto. They heard of Germans and French- men coming to this country and doing the work which the British workmen ought to be doing, I but if they saw the en4husiasm of the foreigners in their work they would feel that we in England were not trying as much as the foreigners to educate ourselves. Unless that condition of things were altered he was afraid the schools of art would not benefit. He (Mr. Millard) attributed whatever success he had achieved to the instruction which he received at the hands of Mr. Aylward. (Applause). When he was at the school the students entertained a great affection for Mr. Aylward, and the sympathy existing between , master and students was keener than he had experienced in any similar institution. They regarded him as much as a friend as a teacher and the success of certain of Mr. Aylward's students proved they were justified in showing him their greatest respect and esteem. In the name of the subscribers he was pleased to hand the present to their old master, with every good wish for his future prosperity. ( Applause). I Mr. MILLARD then presented Mr. Aylward with two solid silver rose bowls with four vases to match. The rose bowls were inscribed : "Presented to Mr. C. B. Aylward by the past and present students of the War- rington School of Art, July 17th, 1907." Mr. AYLWARD, who was cordially received, said he was very pleased indeed to accept that handsome present. It would always be cherished, but he could assure them that nothing was needed to help him to treasure the feelings of sympathy which had existed between the students and himself.(Applause),lt was most difficult for a master to be placed in such a position after so many years,but at the same time it was very gratifying for him to receive that gift. He thanked them from the bottom of his heart and he was particularly grateful to the students who bad taken the matter in hand for the trouble they had gone to in communicating with old students all over the country. Alluding to the ques- tion of art applied to industry, Mr. Aylward referred to the visit which he paid to Venice at the request of the Board of Education. He inspected the glass works, being received with great kindness, and obtained a series of photographs which became the possessions of the Warrington School of Art. Mr. Armstrong, the Director at South Kensington, wished to obtain them for the museum there, but be (Mr. Aylward) said he had acquired them for the school over which he had control. One reason why the glass trade was leaving this country was because the general education of the boys and men had not been properly attended to. Unfortunately he (Mr. Aylward) had only had two apprentices who took a great interest in alas.; work and specimens of their produetions were exhibited some years ago. Mr. Aylwara concluded by again expressing his sincere thanks.

Alderman BENNETT said that was an agreeable oppor- tunity of expressing their regard for Mr. Aylward and their good wishes in the change that was about to take place in his residence. The Warrington School of Art was a school of which he (Alderman Bennett) was extremely proud. Every Warringtonian who knew its history had occasion to be proud of it. He did not think there were many schools which had a more glorious history, and when it came to be written he believed a considerable share of that glory would be reflected on Mr. Aylward to whom they were so sorry to say "Au revoir." They would not say " Good-bye " because they hoped to see him again on many occasions. (Applause). On visiting the Royal Academy this year, he (Alderman Bennett) had the opportunity of inspecting many works by Warring- tonians. The most glorious era of the school was apparently under the regime of Mr. Christmas Thompson, and there were pictures by two of his old pupils—four by Sir Luke rildes, and three by Mr. Harry Woods. But that did not exhaust the good work of former students of the school. There were pictures by four other Warringtoniapp„ and what he wanted to impress upon the meeting was that most if not all of those exhibitors were former pupils of Mr. Aylward. (Applause). There was Mr. Millard, for whom he ventured to prophesy considerable distinction in the noble art to v‘hich he had devoted his life. Mr. Garside, for whom he also predicted fame, had two pictures, and there was a most creditable piece of work by Mr. Wilmot Lunt. There were also specimens of work by Messrs. William and Segar Owen. Mr. William Owen took an i active interest n the School for a long time and probably Mr. Segar Owen was a student there. All this showed that the high traditions were not dying out. VVhen Mr. Aylward retired to the pleasant hills of Sussex it would be a source of satisfaction to him .o feel that his career in Warrington was instrumental in helping others to attain the laurels which he (Alderman Bennett) was sure awaited them. (Applause.) Alderman Bennett concluded by moving a vote of thanks to the , Chairman.

Miss MarTRAm, of South Kensington School of Art, and a former student at the Warrington School,secgnded end endolsed everything that had been said in prCse of Mr. Aylward's work. The vote was heartily accorded.

The CHAIRMAN in returning thanks, said that in its earlier days the institution was not so much a school as a society for mutual improvement for the benefit of those who had leisure and ambition, Later a profes- sional was called in and an academy was instituted in order to instruct those who wished to be trained in the elements of art. That continued during the late Mr. Thompson's time. In the old days there Ras a want of appreciation of the fact that science and manufacture went together, but now those dilletante notions had passed away and science in every departipent was a sine qua non. There was a strong feeling that those who had devoted their lives to art and had gained dis- tinction should be able to direct education and exert an influence for the good of the community without being trammelled with the hard and fast routine of teaching the rudiments which ahould be left in younger hands. He hoped that Mr. Aylward would always look back to his work in Warrington with pleasure and sitisfaction. (Applause). OTHER PRESENTATIONS.

The Pupil Teachers and members of Form VT. assembled at the Art School on Thursday, to present to Mr. Aylward a handsome walking-stick in Malacca cane with engraved silver mounted "Cape" horn handle, and inscribed on the mount "Presented to C. B. Aylward Esq. by the Pupil Teachers and Form VT. of the Warrington Secondary School, July 18th, 1907." Mr. STANT:Er C. JoNEs in a short speech introduced the object of the visit. He slid that athis would be the last opportunity which all present would have of seeing Mr. Aylward, they had assembled there that morning to say !!--Good-bye." He asked Mr. Aylward to accept the present on behalf of the pupil teachers and the scholars nf Form VT. Miss LETTICE WARBURTON then made the p*osentaton.

Mr. AYLWARD rose to reply and received an ovation. He said that he was' extremely thankful for the good wishes expressed. He had always felt that the work with the pupil teachers was the most important wok he had engaged in; because in his opinion it tended to raise the standard of art in elementary schools. (Hear, hear.) The walking-stick would always be his com- panion during his walks in the Sussex lanes, and it

would always remind him of the pleasant time spent among those present. (Applause.) During the week Mr. Aylward has been the recipient of many other beautiful presents from the different Forms in the Secondary school. Mr. W. Fletcher on behalf of Form 5a presented Mr. Aylward with a silver match box. Mr. A. Pemberton on behalf of Form 4 asked the Art master to accept a handsome travelling clock; Form 5b gave Mr. Aylward a silver-mounted inkstand which was presented by Mr. E. Lidiard. Ido hope he will have one of those quiet and very happy lives, in keeping with the beautiful hills and country to which he will shortly be retiring.

HYGIENE AND TEMPERANCE.

Address by Mr. W. N. Edwards.

There was a large gathering at the invitation of Miss Truda Crosfleld at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Monday afternoon, when a lecture was delivered on" Hygiene and Temperance" by Mr. W. N. Edwards, P.C.S., London.

Miss CROSFIELD presided and in introducing Arr. Edwards said that it was not only good for the body that they should place themselves under self restraint but good also for the temperament. (Hear, hear.) Mr. EDWARDS said that for some time medical men had been advocating the teaching of hygiene in the schools. They had for a long time been teaching the youth of the land how their ancestors lived and the question now arose as to whether it would not be better instead to teach the children how to live themselves. The length of human life had as an average very materially increased owing to the fact that they were more and more beginning to recognize the laws of health and to fulfil those laws. Compared with the time when Queen Victoria ascended the throne women were living three years longer and men about two years longer. When they thought of the incalculable value of human life they could see the importance of that increase which had taken place. Asked to account for it, he could only reply that there were laws relating to life and health, and just in proportion as they learned and obeyed those laws so their lives bad been increased. Disease was not a natural state and could be stamped out. In this country at the present moment there was not a single case of cholera, whereas 30 or 40 years ago the disease was not uncommon and was sometimes present in epidemic form. Nor was there a single case of typhus fever and the stamping out of those diseases was due to the fact that they had learned something of their origin, and then instead of applying a cure had taken preventive measures. It had been estimated by a prominent physician that if man could live without infringing a single law of health he could easily attain the ripe old age of 120 and an old age not of decrepitude but of health and vigour, but there were immense numbers growing up who were entirely ignorant of the laws of health. He could not do more than state the bare fact that 20,000 infants died in this country every year as a result of sheer ignorance on the part of the mothers and it was in order to prevent that that it was desired to instruct boys and girls in the elemen- tary principles of hygiene. Hygiene and temperance were synonymous. Where there was intemperance the laws of hygiene were not being observed. Mr. Edwards proceeded to contrast the value of various foods from a scientific standpoint. Dealing with alcohol he pointed out that it was often urged that alcohol stimulated the brain. It has been proved by experiment that to a certain extent that was correct. For a brief period alcohol did lead to increased activity, of the brain but it was always followed by a long period of brain depression. It could not he controverted that water was the most essential factor in the human life and it had also been proved that water and alcolol in all their properties were as far apart as the poles were asunder and no matter what form of life they took they would discover that alcolol is absolutely inimical. (Hear, hear.) The address was freely illustrated by experiments of a simple character ' which were successfully performed. I During the afternoon refreshments were served and musical items were given by Miss Crosfield, Mrs. Haviour and Mrs. Jackson Holmes.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Teachers not to be Insured

A meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee took place at Crewe, on Monday, Dr. Hodgson presiding.

The President of the 136a.rd of Education wrote : "The absence of individual children, whatever be the cause necessarily reduces the average attendance of the school, hut I do not think any serious loss of grant results from exclusion under medical authority—for example, the exclusion of forty children for a week would only involve a loss of about £2 to the local education authority. The method of computing average attendance will, of course, be one of the questions demanding careful consideration in connection with any scheme for reorganising the system of grants; but I have grave doubts whether the system at present in force in secondary schools could be applied without very considerable modification to elementary schools. The Director of Education, in a report on the examina- tions for county junior scholarships, said the work done by the majority of the candidates was disappointinv. Especially was this the case in arithmetic, in which 57 per cent of the candidates obtained not more than 30 per cent of the marks, and not 1 per cent secured 85 per cent of the marks and upwards. The Clerk (Mr. R. Potts) said the County Council had decided that they would not insure teachers under the now Compensation Act, but would accept their liability in reference to the Council schools.

MY DEAR I have been most pleased with the large number of essays sent me. It really has been a difficult task to decide the winners, but, after going through them 'Very carefully, I am for- warding the prizes to Gertie Fisher and Jennie Rushby (will both these Nieces please send me their full address ILA was glad to see so many preferred the country, as I am country-bred my- self. After the office work is finished, I go home miles away from the city. I have my roses and sweet peas and begonias now all in full bloom, and I find plenty of work in keep- ing my flowers—not to mention the vegetable garden—in good order. Now, during July and August, many of you will be away on your holidays, either by the sea or in the dear old country. I want each one to send me a picture postcard of the place; or, if you cannot get aopicture card, send a few words on an ordinary postcard, about the place in which you are staying. For the six best received, I shall give six lovely boxes of Fry's Chocolate. Don't forget that I offered last week six dainty pocket mirrors for the six best contri- butions each week. Well, I must close now, with love to all my inembers. UNCLE NED.

•ej HOLIDAY COMPETITI4ON ESSAYS. Dear Uncle Ned,—l am what you may term a real country Molly. I love the country scenes best—fresh air, animals, sport, games, etc. Of course, the sea is lovely, and I like going to see it once a year. What is more charming at this time of year than a pretty country village, surrounded by trees, corn-fields, grass-fields, flowers, all in full bloom, showing us how God loves and cares- for us? The corn is in ear, the grass is now being cut, and you may hear the noise of the reaping machine, and the honest voices of the husbandmen, calling out, "Gee-up, Dobbin." The cows are now looking sleek, and are grazing in the, fields. At 4.30 we fetch them up, and milk them, and get some lovely, pure milk, which is very nour- ishing. You should see the children here, with their rosy cheeks. Then, again, what nice vegetables we get, and to spare; also fruits to make presqrves. Yes, I think we, country chil- dren have much to be thankful for. I hope I shall be lucky enough to earn one of your prizes some day. We shall all try very hard, especially as teacher allows us to do them at school. GERTIE FISHER.

Dear Uncle Ned,—You are asking us little girls which we prefer—country or the sea. Well, perhaps you know the little village of Anwick, with its pretty church. I live at a farm called The Grange—right out of the village—so I know what country life is like. I think I prefer the sea, with its many wonders. How lovely it looks on a summer day, with the blue water, and boats and steamers sailing about it. One would never suppose it is so treacherous at times, would they? I should love to spend my holi- days there, to have a paddle, and a row in a boat, or go for a sail in a steamer. I go to Skegness for a day in the summer. What fun we have, having rides on donkeys! I think Neddy gets treated very badly for our pleasure.' I think the seaside air seems More bracing than the country, don't you? And it seems to suit invalids. Now, dear Uncle, I will write a longer letter next time, as time is short. JENNIE RUSEBY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—lt has been a long time since I have written to you before, but I know you will excuse me, because I have been busy preparing for examinations. The subject you have chosen for competition is a very nice one, so I thought I would send an essay. You see, although I am not a constUnt contributor, I am a constant reader. GLADYS PENN.

A holiday in the country is more enjoyable to me than any seaside resort. To take a trip in the country is most delight- ful when Nature is at her best. The libdgerows are lined with wild flowers and various berries. How splendid the fields look in the sunshine, when the corn aid poppies are waving in the breeze! AD To walk down the shady lanes, with the birds singing overhead, and the leaves rustling be- neath our feet, is very_ pleasing to the ear. The reapers will soon be reaping the golden harvest, and with this signal comes the fun of harvest-time. GLADYS PENN.

,g a pieet, of ttrj own this week. I like both the seaports and the country, but I think the country is the best. My favourite place is Kidderminster. There are several splendid walks over there.

I go in July for my holiday. In that —month there are the flowers out All the houses over there have large gardens, with beautiful flowers growing. There are several beautiful places, such as Habberley Valley, Brinton Park, Stour- port, and Bewdley, and there are several others. Habberley Valley is a splendid valley: it goes down in a hollow; there are several rocky parts, and in some places it is full of trees. There is a large piece of water at Brinton Park.; all around it there are beautiful, large trees, and at the side of the pavement there are all kinds of flowers.

At Stourport there is a large bridge, which hundreds of people go across. There is a large hotel by the side of the river. At Kidderminster, there is a large Town Hall, and a beautifth church, called St. Mary's Church. There is another church, called Baxter Church, and near the church there is a statue, called Sir Rowland Hill Statue. Last year, when I was there, my cce.:ziP arid I went gathering blackberries; all hedgen there are blackberries growing. ELIZABETH STOCKS.

Dear Uncle Ned.—The inhabitants of a' town which is not a seaside resort have very little opporttmity of spendi-,,2 a day by the sea. It is quite r. 'eht to see little boys and girls paddlin_w _ad also digging castles. Some people prefer the ferry- boat rather than the steamer, so they row round the tier for an hour or so. Generally, when the fide is in, the people assemble on the pier, where there is a band playing. At some seaside places some men dress up and give a nigger entertainment, which is another source of amusement. Their funny songs and the way they express their words cause roars of laughter. M. H.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l love to spend my holidays in the country. There, with my parents and brother, I could go for long walks over the hills and down in the valleys, running- after the beautiful butterflies and moths. Then go and sit under some shady tree to read, or go and gather leaves of different kinds to press in a book. Another day we would go near a marsh, and get some frogs, which we would tickle, to make them jump about, If we were staying at a farm-house we would go into a field, and sit in a ring while we had our meals. _

BENNY FARD.AR.

De* Uncle Ned,—This is the first time of writing to you, and I hope you will accept me as one of your nephews. There is a little girl in our school writing to you, and she has put "Dear Auntie Ned,' ,nd we are all laughing at the present tim€llll :ou wish to know what place I should, like to visit. Well, both places are nice, hut, you see, .I have lived in the country all my life, so I 'think I must say the sea. I have visited Skegness; and have stood watching the water with wonder—l might say awe—thinking how wonderful it is to keep its place, and not flood the land. How nice, too, it is to have a bathe, and feel the waves gradually coming in. Our clergyman is going to take the Choir to a seaside place this month, and I am looking forward to it with great pleasure. Now, dear Uncle Ned, I must wish you good-bye.

LESLIE PADLEY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—As we take in your paper, I have asked my teacher if I could write to you, as I see other little girls do. I must tell you I am a real country girl. I love the sea, but I love the country as well. You ought to be here now everything is in full bloom. The laay is cut, and we can have a good romp. If yot wake now at about 2.30 in the morning, you will hear a concert among the dear little birds, each one trying which can sing best. This little village is very pretty, and all around us we can see lovely trees, and churches in the midst with lovely spires. In the evening how quiet and peaceful it all seems—just as a day should end. MAUDE BROTHWELL. —+—. MILK, OH!

Mr. Joners, who intended taking his wife out for a drive one day, asked his milkman, who had a very spirited horse, for the loan of the same"; which request was granted. However, Mr. Jones was not a good driver, and had great difficulty in managing the horse, which at last became ungovernable, and, tr;_+he horror of Mrs. Jones, bolted with them. Mr. Jones did not know what to do, and. a serious accident seemed unavoidable, when, all of a sudden, Mr. Jones, remembering the capacity in which the horse was used, and 'calling out with a stentorian voice, "Milk, oh! milk, oh l" the horse stopped instantly, to their great joy, at this familiar cry, and Mr. and Mrs. Jones got home safely, without any further incident, save that when they, returned home in the evening, on passing a pump in the neighbourhood, the horse would not stir an inch until Mr. Jones got down and worked the pump handle a dozen times, after which operation it moved on directly; and to finish off the day's pleasure, it stopped at all the customers of the milkman in the road where Mr. Jones lives, his house being at the further end.

Old Gentleman (to ticket-collector): "Your life, my young friend, I imagine, is a laborious one and full of trials?"

Collector: "Yes, sir; but there are moments when it is filled with peace and happiness." Old Gentleman: "When your wages are paid, I suppose?" Collector: "No, sir; when I slam the gate in the face of somebody who is in a hurry."

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am going to tell you which I should like to go to best—seaside or the country. I would rather go to the seaside, because I live in the country, and would rather hive a change. A change is nicer than seeing the same thing over and over again. At the seaside I should see the pier, the sea, the beauti- ful shops, the large eating-houses, lots of big houses, and such a lot of things that I cannot mention them all. Besides all this, I could fish, paddle, bathe, watch the tide come in, and numerous other things, which, I think, would be very enjoyable. What fun it is, too, building sand castles, houses, etc., on the sands, when the tide is out, and digging deep holes. I often envy the Little boys and girls when I think of them having a nice'paddle this hot weather. I will come to a close, hoping you and I may have the pleasure of going.

LILIAN LUNN.

Professor C— had gone to spend the even- ing at a friend's house. When he was about to leave, it was raining very heavily, wherefore the hostess kindly offered him accommodation. Suddenly the guest disappeared, nobody knowing what had become of him, and the family were about to retire for the night, when Professor C— walked in, as wet as a drowned rat. He had been home to fetch his night-shirt!

1 ELECTROPLATING WITH CADMIUM. - As cadmium can now be had at a reatiOn- able price, it may be of interest, says a Writer in the De-utsche, Metailindustrie Zei- tung, to know that th,2re may ba obtained an aUsolutelv satisfactory platg therewith by Up to a r-acent date, er, ..atf2nlpts in this direction met with

gaivan:c deposition b.Owe

• no success. Cadmium is soft, has no caustic action, does not readily dim under the action j of vapours containing sulphuretted hydro- gen, and its colour is as white as that of tin, although not so- white as that of silver. As compared with the latter metal, however, the galvanic deposit is harder and permits a highcr polish,. Experiments shew that for thi3 purpose a solution .of cadmium car- bonate is best, and that the most favourable

results may be obtained by the employment of such a solution. To make this latter, a solution of any soluble cadmium salt in water is treated with sodium carbonate.

DANGERS OF THE HOUSE-FLY. The common house-fly is not quite so inno- cent as he sometimes looks.\ Dr. Barras, a Scots bacteriologist, has been conducting a 52rie5 of experiments as to the part played by the house-fly in the dissemination of in- fectious disease, and has published the re- sults. Several flies, he says, were made to crawl over freshly - prepared agar-agar, con- tained in sterile petri-dishes. The dishes were then placed in an incubator at the nor- mal temperature of the body, and at the end •of twenty-four hours numerous colonies of

micro-orianisms had appeared on the sur- face of the media. On microscopical examina- Cori, these colonies were seen to consist of, amongst others, growths of the micro-or- ganisms associated with the presence of sewage and decomposing organic matter. The gralt increase in the cases of diarrhoea, he contends, was also intimately related to the advent of the house-fly.

LANCASHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Higher Education.

Tha Lanoashire Education Committee at their meet- ing at Preston on Monday, over which Sir Henry F. Hibbert presided, approved the estimates for higher leducation in non-county boroughs and urban districts. The estimated expenditure for the year ending March Si 1998, was £92051. Towards this the County Council contributed £39,726, the estimate in the budget being £35,000, so that there was an estimated surplus of £5,274. Last year the higher education expenditure was £89,430, so for this year there is an increase of 0,621. In presenting the report of the Higher Education Sub-committee, Sir Henry Hibbert congratulated the Agricultural Sub-committee on the steady progress they were making in their work. The decision to give the students instruction in the judging of farm stock was a decided step in the right direction, while it had been decided to establish two centres in the county during the winter session for giving instruction in horticulture to elementary school teachers at a fee of 55., so that that the children in rural schools could be instructed in. gardening. Referring to the new secondary and technical school at Waterfoot, he said the cost of £26,000 was undoubtedly great, but it had been decided after careful consideration and was the alternative to building technical schools at Bacup and Rawtenstall and a day Secondary school at Waterfoot with separate staffs. - -

The report wee adopted. GOAT:kRICMG. _ _

Miss Assheton sucie-sTe-d-Wai—tlie Agricultural Com- mittee should take into consideration the advisability of keeping goats. The question was not so much an agricultural one as a hygienic one in relation to the supply of milk for children. The Committee referred the matter to the Agricultural Committee. Major Greg (Turton), respecting complaints to the Cotton Industry Sub-committee from six students,whose cotton scholarships would shortly terminate, as to the difficulty of obtaining situations, said it was a great pity that Lancashire manufactuers did not support the committee's efforts to find work for these students. He thought it would be found that the students were going to prove exceedingly valuable members of our industrial community. Mr. W. 1. Riley (Briercliffe) thought inquiries would prove that most of the students who had attended the Manchester School had secured good or promising appointments. (Hear, hear).

===== 0002601_19070720_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
EfTRSARS AND TEACHING.

Sir Henry Hibbert, referring to the Board of Educa- tion's new regulations forcandidates desiring to become teachers, reported on his interview with Mr. Morant, of the Board of Education, who held out no hope of allow- ing bursars to teach the whole of the week instead of only four days. To make up for this loss of bursars' services in schools, Sir Henry moved that the monition that males receive .£4O and females £3O be rescinded, and that the salaries be males £26 and females £2O. Mr. B. South (teachers' representative) remarked that the new scheme was full of difficulties, and its adoption should be postponed. He wished the Board of Educa- tion could be buried for ten years, and leave them to become their own authority. (Laughter). Archdeacon Fletcher: Twenty years. (Renewed laughter and assent). Dr. Snape (director of education) observed that it would be unfair to some young people to postpone the matter, as they had given up their pupil teacherships to become bursars, and the vacant pupil teacherships thus createdhad already been filled. Sir Henry Hibbert said he did not hesitate to say that ultimately all the period for practical school teaching woult disappear. The motion was carried.

PLASANT PARAGRAPHS.

Mrs. Gadde : "The —Lowmans celebrated their silver wedding last night, didn't they?" Mrs. Flyte : "Oh, no; I saw all the pre- sents." 'Eh? What do 'you mean?" "It was more like a silver-plated wedding."

Teacher: "And why are you so late, 'Tommy?" Tommy: "If you please, teacher, it is mother's washing day; she lost the lid of the copper, so I have been sitting on the top to keep the steam in !"

Susie had tried the teacher's patience sorely, and when the latter looked up and saw the little girl eating toffee, with her feet sprawling into the aisle, she said : "Susie Ambler, take that toffee out of your mouth and put .your feet in !"

"Tomn I'm going to punish you severely.' ".What for, pa?" "Now, don't try that innocenct2 game. I know all the had things you've done to-day.", "No, you. don't; pa. You don't know Where I hid the strap ,you whack me with."

Bobby: "I don't know; but she's standing in front of the mirror anAl blushing and saying, 'Oh, Mr. Softly--,er—this is so sudden.' "

Host: "I'm sorry .to •send you out in such a blustering night Os this, old fellow." Guest: "It is raining Pretty hard. I say couldn't you lend me your umbrella?" "Cer- tainly; and—er—l think I'll walk .borne with you myself. I really ne,ul a little exercise."

The boy had been thoughtful for a short space of time. Presently he looked up and burst forth with: "I say, father, if you call the tail of a sheep a leg, how many legs Will the sheep have?" "Five," said the parent. "No," %plied the youngster, "it would only have four. Calling the tail a leg wouldn't make it one." He has since worked his jokes off on his younger biether.

"I say, Kate," said Johnny to his elder sister, would you like to have some nice chocolate?" "Yes," replied Kate. "Well, if you'll buy me some I'll give you half.'!

Mr. T. W. Russell, M.P., is the proprietor d a temperance hotel in Dublin.

During his early struggles Mr. Carnegie's recreation was fishing, and he is still an ardent angler.

King George of Greece can playtall kinds of tunes on handbells and wineglasses of different shapes. . . .

The Earl of Desart. King's Proctor and Direc- tor of Public Prceecutions, is an experienced amateur actor.

Lord Charles Beresford is a man of many hobbies, which include boat-building, garden- ing, house-building, turning, and carpentry.

At a public dinner recently Mr. Augustine Birrell remarked: " I have no desire to culti- vate my garden. I do not like stooping. I would rather go without vegetables than grow my own."

Although not the richest man living—in this respect he is second to Mr. Rockefeller—Mr. Pierpont Morgan, the American multi-million- aire—is said to have at his command more ready cash than any person in the world.

As Master of the Horse, the Earl of Seftoh enjoys a salary of .-C2,500 a year. A perquisite that go`es with the post, and is much appre- ciated, is the use of a Royal carriage and pair, a coachman and footman, and a horse for riding purposes whenever wanted.

Some few years ago, when Lord Hawke had been repeatedly losing the toss for Yorkshire in cricket matches, he received a " lucky tsix- pence" from a Yorkshire lady, with a request that he would use it %hen spinxiing the coin. The very first time he used the sixpence it gave him the choice of innings, and it eeems to have since brought Yorkshire more than utsual" luck" in the toss.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

A Housewife and the Smoke Nuisance.

S,ir,—Will you allow an ex-Warrington housewife to make articulate what every housekeeper who lives in Warrington feels in regard to the smoke nuisance? Happily for mysell, I have been able, by removing from Warrington to the country, to escape the expense and disadvantages which are due to the smoke polluted atmosphere. Since making the change I have been able to realise to the full the advantages and pleasures of housekeeping where the house is surrounded by pure air, and where nothing obstructs the sunshine. My expenditure for laundry and dry-cleaning is about half what it used tobe, and as a result I confidently antici- pate everything will last longer. Window curtains here—at the end of five months—look fresher and cleaner than they did at the end of three weeks in War- rington. In our own rooms, in cases where the windows were open day and night, after one week there was a thick black line of smuts and dust where the curtains had filtered the incoming air. The smuts were of a greasy character, and any attempt to remove them resulted in smears impossible to remove, like dry country dust. White counterpanes I here use twice as long as I did in town; and pillow cases etc., never get really dirty; whereas in Wai rington smuts and dust used to settle thickly ,on pillows and toilet covers; often between morning and evening dirt would gather so thickly on inside window ledges as to necessitate removal by brush. Here we can open the windows day and night, and admit nothing except the glorious fresh air. We and the children keep cleaner, we can without fear wear white suits and dresses. When the children went out in Warrington they generally returned with smeared smuts on their faces and garments, and with nostrils choked with dirt. Then in addition to the freedom from dirt on dress and person, we can now rejoice in the unimpeded glorious sunshine. Here when there is 9.ny sunshine we get the full benefit of it. In Warrington as a rule the sunlight came to our home robbed of half its brightness. We only got what the smoke ladened atmosphere failed to absorb or deflect. We all agree that we feel cleaner in house, body, and mind since we left the town and its its smoke behind.—Yours etc., AN EX-WARRINGTON HOUSEWIFE

===== 0002601_19070720_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
The Education of our Children.

Sir,—One of the most painful and, at the same time, most convincing proof of the incompatibility of modern Christianity with the teaching of Jesus Christ and His apostles is to be found in the attitude of the various sects in reference to the religions education of the children of the nation. A few years ago the late Con- servative Government passed an Education Bill greatly in favour of the Church party, and the measure so pro- voked the great body of Nonconformists that to a large extent they met it with passive resistance. The present Radical Government which came into power in 1906 made a somewhat curious attempt to undo the legisla- tion of their predecessors, but the Lords refused to pass their Bill, and therefore it had to be abandoned. Another equally unjust and impotent measure to deal with the subject is now before the public, and it may be safely predicted that it will share the fate of the previous attempt to deal with the impossible. All these proceedings-are a scandal to a country calling itself Caristian,andlarise from the melancholy fact that after fourteen centuries of preaching we have net yet arrived at a common Christianity. The Gospel accord- ing to its Divine Founder and His immediate followers is, unfortunately, very much relegated to the back- ground, and gospels according to Canterbury, Rome, Wesley, Calvin, &c., &c., seem to be the guiding and •ruling powers among the various religious denomina- tions which presume to call themselves Churches oi Christ. How impossible then it is for any Government or other power to devise and carry out a system of religious education that would satisfy the various sectarian combatants! Besides, the duties of a Govern- ment are secular and not spiritual, and they have no power to make hr unmake laws for the col:Sciences of men. Indeed, when we consider what the House of Commons is, one is astonished that it should ever attempt religious legislation at all ! That assembly is composed of men of all creeds and men who have no creeds but their own ambition and selfishness, and the idea that such men could make proper laws for the religious education of our children is most absurd and impracticable. Two courses are, however, within the power of our legislators, and either of them would be more consist- ent with common sense and equity than making laws for religion. They could establish and endow all the sects and thus create a Babel that would eclipse the tower building in the plain of Shinar in daring and dis- regard of the laws of Christ. By such legislation they would become responsible to God for giving counte- nance and support to a congeries of sects which claim to be Christian but differ in many ways from the plain and simple teaching of the Lord and His apostles. This kind of legislation would, I daresay, meet the views of the sectarians, but they would be bold and reckless men indeed who would dare to degrade the Gospel and dishonour its Author by such a system. The other course open to the government is to leave religion severely alone. They were not elected to pass laws for the regulation and enforcement of Christian ethics, but to look after the mundant, affairs of the nation. If they will dabble with education let them see to it that every child in the kingdosa is thoroughly instructed in secular knowledge to enable him to take an intelligent part in the various industries of life and thus become a useful member of society. And this knowledge should be supplemented by the golden rule of doing unto others as we would have others do unto us. But with creeds and (catechisms our rulers haven° business. Religious educa- tion, like charity, should begin at home. The duty of parents is to train up their children in the way they should go. But as, unfortunately, many of them are unable to do this owing to the unsatisfactory state of modern Christianity, the ministers of religion might be expected to take an interest in the matter, and look after the spiritual welfare of the rising generation. The work would he congenial to their taste and occupation, and judging from the fact that most of them are only actively employed for one day in the week, they would have ample time for such a philanthropic purpose. The various creeds would thus be upheld and propagated without any noise and bitterness, and without calls upon the government for money.—Yours truly, lONA. July, 1907.

LETCHWORTH: THE GARDEN CITY.

SOME FIRST IMPRESSIONS.

[Br A MEMBER OF THE WOHMT'S LIDERA.L ASSOCIATION].

How many residents of Latchford have journeyed to Warrington by the 6.55 a.m. car? Certainly last Saturday was the first time I have done it, and almost without breakfast, too. I heard of one lady who, after a big drink of milk, ate her breakfast as she was going along the King's highway, and of two others who drank "boiling tea" and missed the car through trying to fortify themselves with ham and eggs. But they caught the train, 7.30 at the Central, thanks to the aid of a motor-car, which was just available, but not in good working order. Rumour has it (of course, she's a "lying jade") that these two ladies were seen pushing the motor-car on its way to the Central Station. I was much interested in the passengers by the 6.55 tram-car. They were mostly young, boys and girls from about fifteen to twenty years old, all looking more or less sleepy and indifferently washed. One young lady, presumEibly in the habit of "touching up" her cc-uplexion, had a dab of powder on each cheek and another on the end of her nose. Bridge-street and Horsemarket-street , looked as they never looked before ; the shops all closed, hardly a person to be seen except newspaper vendors, old women and young ones, men and 'boys wheeling "Examiners " and' Guardians" in boxes on wheels and decrepit perambulators. I don't see my local papers until much later in the day, and so strong is habit that though I was dreamily conscious that I should want the local news some time during the day, it never occurred to me to buy it in the street. It was not until I saw my fellow-passengers 'in the train unfold their damp, ink-smelling papers that I realized how stupid I had been. I always was troubled with after-wit. But there was no lack of literature in that train. We hadn't been aboard many minutes before we were presented with a Garden City Souvenir by Mr. Lichfield, the obliging organising secretary of the Garden City Tenants, Ltd., and at intervals during the day circulars &c., appeared on our tables including two most appetising menus, for lunch and dinner. We reached Letchworth about 12.50. It had rained most of the way, but the Garden City was bathed in sunshine, and with the exception of a sprinkling later, continued so the whole afternoon. For convenience sake the passengers were divided into two contingents—" A" and "B," and supplied with differently tinted programmes. I can't say how many of us there were. It was a " special " from Liverpool, calling at Warrington, Oldham and Man- chester. There were many familiar faces on the platform that morning, principally Liberals, including the Chairman of the Association, and one well-known Conservative —Alderman Bennett. Mr. Crosfield, M.P. motored down from London to Letchworth. He was evidently pleased to see so many of his constituents —the pleasure was mutual. Wearing a ruddy brown suit, and tanned to the same colour he looked well. After listening to an explanation of the scheme from Mr. Vivian, M.P., the "A " contingent were driven off in lakes, waggonettes, landaus &c., round Ithe estate, while the "B "contingent walked to the Garden City Press Works for lunch and vice versa. The Gatden City Tenants Limited, believe in "booming" On complimenting one of the officials on the success of the undertaking he replied :—" Oh yes, if you wheel a barrow up a plank get it in the news- papers." Advertisers please note We were " A's " and after listening to a couple of addresses from Mr. Vivian, and a number of questions and answers re co-partnership Housing, we were afforded the oppor- tunity of looking over two of the Society's houses, already tenanted. That was what we wanted feminine curiosity would be satisfied with nothing less. They were small, for small families,containing three and four bed-rooms and bath-room. One ix particular pleased me. It was a detached house prettily gabled,with an artistic entrance furnished with seats, semi-circular in shape, under a verandah and half the floor space was taken up in one long living- room with a window at each end and an ornamental arch about one-third from the end to break the long line. The other half contained a snuggery and kitchens. I would like to have broulzht it back with me and planted it in Latchford. One of the more architecturally-minded " A's " said some of the gables were wrong. I was glad I didn't know that. To me they were very pretty, and to see all those red roofs, grey walls, and bright green doors and window frames gleaming in the sun reminded one of the Swiss chalets. Every house is gardened round, the grates and windows are most modern, accommodation is ample, and the rents are reasonable. Four years ago there wasn't a brick on the estate, now the population is 3,000, and increasing by leaps and bounds. The city is intended ultimately to find homes for 30,000 people. The residents are apparently disciples of the -‘ simple life." No hats and sandals, skimpy skirts and bare arms and necks being general. The old part of Letchworth is nicely undulating and well- wooded; and five years hence (say) when the creepers are grown, gardens well established land the newness of everything mellowed down it, will be most picturesque. What should we in Warrington think if our roads were bordered by a strip of grass several feet wide and alongside that a garden-bed about the same width, with, in some cases, hundreds of rose trees, and in others Sweet Williams in gay profusion,lapins, snap-dragons and pansies galore? And shouldn't we think the Millennium had come if our neighbour just left them alone and didn't help himself to that which isn't his'n ? Why, a particular pansy root, not a hundred miles from Latchford, was appropriated the other moping, early, and at Easter not a daffodil is allowed to bloom within a few yards of the garden gate. And when we hear of °tie man growing ferns in his bath and another using his as a tank for gold fish it makes one despair of ever getting people to realize the necessity of cleanliness:and sanitation. But lam wandering from the Garden City, and that we didn't do until 6.30 p.m. It took us six hours to reach Warrington, somewhat tired and sleepy, but all of one mind—that it was worth it. There is nothing more effectual for shortening a long railway journey than congenial company,and a first-class dining saloon with the dining included. Seven of us were the guests of Miss Crosfield for the day, than whom a more genial, charming hostess it has never been my good- fortune to meet.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Sil cock —Gleave.

The Warrington Parish Church was the scene of a very pretty wedding on Tuesday, when Mr. /4 arold Alfred Fairhurst Silcock, eldest son of Mr. Alfred John Silcock, Bold-street, was married to Miss Edith Coningsby Gleave,eldest daughter of Ur. JamesGleave, Springfield-street. The bride and bridegroom are well known in the town, and a large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Vicar of Holy Trinity, officiated. The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired. Ske was attended by three brides- maids—Miss L. M. Gleave, Miss Ida W. Silcock, and Miss M. Gleave. Mr. F. M. Darby, Mus. Bac., pre- sided at the organ and played choice selections, includ- ing Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." As the bridal party left the Church, the bells rang out a merry peal.

===== 0002601_19070720_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Bibby—Heald.

A pretty wedding took place at Emmanuel Church on Thursday, when the marriage of Miss Elizabeth Alice Heald, daughter of the late Mr. John (Heald, Church street, and Mr. Wm. Bibby, second son of Mr. William Bibbv, Thyrine-street, was solemnized ,by the Rev. F. Vahan. There was a large attend- ance at the Church to witness the ceremony. The I bride looked charming in a dress of cream silk crepelle, with a veil of Brussels net surmounted by a wreath of orange blossom. She carried a shower bouquet of .lilies and orange blossom. The brides- maids were Miss A. Bibby, sister of the bridegroom Miss E. Landon, niece of the bride, and Miss 8., Dickens, niece of the bridegroom, Miss Bibby wore a dress of embroidered muslin and a crinoline hat and carried a bouquet of pink carnations, whilst the younger bridesmaids wore dresses of white muslin, with pink sashes, and carried bouquets of sweet peas. Mr. H. Bibby was the best man. Miss N. Gandy presided at the organ and rendered several appropriate selections. The reception was held at the house of Mr. Bibby after which the happy couple left for Black- pool for the honeymoon, the bride travelling in a costume of pale blue cloth. Many beautiful presents were received.

1,700 NAIL HOLES.

During the hearing of a claim in the Blackpool County Court on Wednesday against the tenant of a house for the alleged dilapidated state in which the premises had been left, it was stated that there were 1,700 nail hgles in the walls.

THE SCHEME.

The new local Company which bears the name of "Warrington Garden Suburbs, Limited," has this week been duly registered and its Prospectus, which has for some time past been eagelly antici- pated, will be found in our advertising columns. We are glad to find that the preliminary work of several months has resulted in a document so interesting and so suggestive. So long ago as 1888,tbe Garden City idea was hinted at in the first issue of" Sun- rise," and it was definitely suggested in a series of articles published under the title of "Warrington : As it was, as it is, and as it mihht be," in 1892. In 1900, Alder- Bennett, one of the writers, elaborated the scheme still further in " The Dream of a Warringtonian," and proposed the forma- tion of a company with•this end in view, and he, together with Mr. C. E, Parker (who, as our readers are aware, has taken a keen interest in the question), and Canon Stevenson, the Chairman of the Beautiful warrington Society and the founder of the City 'Beautiful Conference, have been the prime movers in the formation of the present Company. They have been fortunate enough to enlist the sympathy and support of some of the most influential people in the neighbourhood, and probably no local Company has ever appealed to the public with a stronger Board of Directors. In addition to the three gentlemen already mentioned, it includes the names of Captain George Crosfield,Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. William Long, Mr. F. W. Monks, Mr. J. Charlton Parr, Mr. Henry Roberts, and Mr. W. Peter Rylands. Mr. C. E. Parker is the Chairman, and Alderman Bennett the Secretary. The Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Company, Ltd., have been appointed bankers for the Company, the Solicitors are Messrs. Henry Greenall and Cb. and Mr. F. R. Roberts, and the auditor Mr. Louis A. Voisey, chartered accountant, whilst the Registered Office is situate at Market Gate Chambers.

The project, in its present form, was first definitely launched on the Ist Decem- ber last, in a letter signed by Canon Stevenson, Alderman Bennett, Mr. Long, Mrs. Mary Garnett, Mr. Parker, and Miss Trada Crosfleld, and they, together with Mr. E. James Hall, another of the earliest supporters of the movement, are the seven signatories to the Memorandum and Articles of Association. On Friday, the 7th December, a public meeting was held at the Town Hall to consider the question, Mr. Crossley Greenwood,Organis- ing Secretary of the Co-partnership Tenants Housing Council, addressing an influential audience. The support of some thirty well known Warringtonians had been secured as the result of a preliminary canvass, and they met at the Old Academy, on Friday, the 21st December, and unanimously decided to form a WarrinFton Garden Suburbs Company. A Provisional Com- mittee was appointed, consisting of Canon Stevenson, Alderman Bennett, Councillor J. J. Nield, Captain Cros- field, Mr. William Bibby, Mr. Benjamin Farrar, the sites have been selected with a due regard to improvement of the Company's estates or in _ Mr. Linnssus Greening, Mr. E. REMINISCENCES OF A MOTOR TOUR. "All work and no play "Make's Jack a dull boy." Not rhyme exactly, your readers will admit, but still true. IA. well known author once wrote "Three men in a boat." This is a narrative of the experiences of four men in a motor car. Nothing humorous is ; intended as the writer is known to most Warring- tonians as being to some extent ' devoid of wit, probably owing to the fact that he has Scotch blood in hia veins. But as 7a guide to aspiring motorists the following story may be useful. It is surprising how many Britishers know so little of their native land. Many have added to their geographical knowledge by cycling through 1 - districts hitherto unknown to them. Others perchance too old, or infirm (or probably lazy) have 'taken advantage of the much abused motor car. This, when used as a medium for pleasure, an not for rushing about the country and interfering with the comfort of His Majesty's subjects (who have, when all is said and done, a preferential right to the use of the roads) is most useful. Pardon this degression—Deponents state (to nse a legal phrase)that Warrington was left upon a certain Friday evening at about 7.15. The first- stop was made at Whitchurch, where the party halted for the night. One of the company being dead tired declined to accompany his associates in their "nightly prowl." When, however, this particular individual had waited until 10.15 p.m. he considered it was about time he had also a walk round. Happening upon two stalwart and able-bodied Guardians of the Peace, he asked them what time the houses (within tke meaning of the Act) closed in Cheshire where- upon he was promptly reminded that he was in Shropshire. The curtain fell and the discomfited visitor retired. Saturday morning, whilst awaiting breakfast, two of the more religiously inclined visited the ancient Parish Church, in which they were most interested. Three volumes of "Fox's Book of Martyrs" were seen padlocked and chained to an old oak desk. The interior of the Church, the ancient font and the stained glass windows were all worthy of attention. From Whitchurch the party proceeded to Shrewsbury, where they halted for probably fifteen minutes, taking the opportunity of inspecting the magnifi- cent Market Hall. From Shrewsbury to Leominster, where they lunched and afterwards accomplished the 27 mile journey to Worcester via Bromyard. They were informed that this was a very hilly country and discovered it was a veritable switchback, but all con- cerned agreed it was the loveliest Fart of England they had yet visited. The roads were perfect; there was a thorough absence of smoke as we see it in the more northern counties; roses and other flowers were blooming in profusion; hop-vines and apple and pear orchards were seen at their best, and every house, from the mansion of the great to the cottage of the poor, had evidently had the great- est care and attention bestowed upon it, even the yews being ti immed in a large variety of shapes classical and otherwise. Upon arrival at Worcester a temporary stay was made and the ancient Cathedral &c., inspected. From Worcester to Stratford-on-Avon where ShakeSpeare's home and Ann Hathaway's cottage were visited, and then on to Warwick where the party remained for the night. Leaving Warwick on Sunday mornington Leamington and Kenilworth were visited, and then the journey was continued on to Coventry. Five miles outside Coventry, and from there to the town many thousands of cyclists were met, leading the party to suppose that it was some fete day. Upon arrival, however, it wee discovered that it was quite a usual thing on every fine Sunday in the summer to see this .exodus. • Birmingham was taken en route, then through lovely eountry to Newcastle-under-Lyne, via Stone and Stoke. From Newcastle to Congleton and then to Knutsford, where the pa4t3T had tea and home, Warrington being reached by eight o'clock. For intending motorists or cyclists who have time at their disposal, no better route could be suggested, ,granted fine weather. It•opens out to the majority of people scenes of which they had perhaps not even dreamed.

BOLD-STREET ORGAN.

MORE RECITALS.

The members of the congregation of Bold-street Wesleyan Church are thoroughly pleased with the fine organ which has been installed and the selection com- mittee have been warmly congratulated upon their excellent choice. The instrument possesses excep- tional beauty of tone and great power, and its splendid qualities have been further demonstrated this week in enjoyable recitals. The services on Sunday were of a festal character befitting such a red letter occasion in the history of the Church. The preacher was the Rev. Samuel T. House, of Liverpool, g hose interesting dis- courses were liatened to by large congregations. Mr. W. H. Payton/Mus Bac., presided at the organ a each service. The choir gave exceedingly creditable interpretations of the anthems "Bless the Lora 0 Illy Saul" (Payton) "Te Deum in F,, (Joason), and "The Wilderness "(Goss). Mrs. Arthur Booth sang "I will extol Thee 0 God" (Costa) most effectively. At the close of the evening service Mr. Payton gave an organ recital which afforded the congregation considerable pleasure. The companion pieces by Wolstenholme are particularly pleasing compositions and Mr. Payton's sympathetic rendering brought out their beauties to the full. -
