GREAT FIRE AT WARRINGTON. £20,000 Damag'f! at Messrs. R. A. Naylor's Works,

There was great excltement in Warrington late on Wednesday night when it became known that a fire had broken out at the works of Messrs. R. A. Naylor, Ltd., Howley, Warrington. The red glare in the sky which could be seen for miles around made it only too evident that the outbreak wad a serious one, and when the ringing of the fire bell was followed by the weird sounds of the hooter at Messrs. • Crosfield's works people were soon hurrying in thousands from all parts of the town to the scene of the fire. How the outbreak originated is a mystery. It appears that the works were left apparently safe at about half. past eight, but shortly before ten o'clock a lady and gentleman were passing the vicinity and noticed flames issuing from a block of buildings. One of the works firemen was informed and the alarm was raised, with the result that many of the workmen were quickly on the spot and a pump and 'hose pipe were got to work. In the meantime the Warrington Fire Brigade had been summoned and turned out with commendable promptitude under the commarfd of Superintendent Turner and Deputy- Superintendent Turner. The joiners' department where the fire broke out was by this time a mass of flame and it was deemed necessary to call out Messrs. Crosfield's brigade who soon responded, Superintendent Gallon being in charge. Three fire engines were brought into use and owing to the close proximity of • the river, an unlimited supply of water was available. It was soon realized that the block of buildings comprising four joiners' shops and two machine shops was doomei to destruction, for the fire was raging furiously. The flames rose to a tremendous height and myriads of sparks shot upwards. The heat, too, was intense. The dry timber fell an easy prey to the flames,and the efforts of the brigades had to be directed to securing adjoining parts of the works. The firemen worked heroically and too much praise camiot be accorded to them, for they stcceeded in practically confining the flames to the departments above mentioned. In this they were aided by the weather conditions, for had there been a strong wind nothing could have saved the rest of the property, and houses in the vicinity would also have been imperilled. The roof of the joiners' department fell in with a great crash and soon a wall collapsed, falling on to the iron roof of an adjacent machine shop and smash- ing it in. The fall of this wall was fortunate for it had the effect of smothering some of the fire, and making the brigades' task easier. At one time a large shed in which a great quantity of timber is stored got alight, but the firemen worked so hard that the flames were extinguished and the building was saved, although some of the beams were charred and the• glass roof was considerably damaged. There was much risk to the firemen from masses of falling . debris, but fortunately no-one was hurt. Before half- past eleven the fire was well under control, but the brigades were at work all night playing on the smouldering ruins, while the Warrington brigade remained on duty on Thursday morning. A scene of devastation met the gaze of visitors to the works on Thursday. The four joiners' shops and two machine shops were completely gutted, only a mere framework remaining. Costly machinery was destroyed and a great quantity of finished work, of considerable value, as well as stacks of timber in the yard, suffered a similar fate. Many workmen also lose' their valuable tools which are kept on the premises. It is indeed a cause for congratulation that the ear- rounding buildings were saved and in several import- ' ant departments work is proceeding as usual. The offices of the firm were not touched by the fire, so that the books were not. damaged. The horses were also saved, workmen promptly removing them from the stables. It •is hoped that those employes who would in the ordinary course have been thrown out of employ- ment, will soon be found work in other parts of the premises. The damage, which is ; estimated at £20,000, is covered by insurance. The police arrangements f)r de ding with the crowd wer3 excellent, being under the personal supervision of the new Chief Constable (Mr. N. Nicholls). .1.••••••••••••••• ANOTHER FIRE. A SHOCK FOR THE " DAILY MAIL"

Mr. W. H. Lever, M.P., Gets £50,000, Damages.

THE YELLOW PRESS HUMBLED.

Quite unexpectedly, and, indeed, somewhat sensation- ally, the libel action by Lever Brothers, Limited, against the Associated Newspapers, Limited, in regard to articles in the '• Daily Mail" and "Evening News" on the soap combine, came to an end at the Assizes on Wednesday.

Mr. Rufus Isaacs announced that the defence was unreservedly withdrawn, he tendered a complete apology, and offered £50,000 damages, a record sum in a libel action. Judgment was taken for this amount, and the case came to an end.

Mr. Lever, who was in the box during the whole of the previous day, proved a splendid witness. He gave the eager Court the impression, not only of a man with a strong case, but of a man who was determinbd not to rob it of any of its strength by answering more than he was asked, or indulging in any of those irrelevances by which a garrulous witness often harms the cause he seeks to help. Mr. Rufus Isaacs, the leading counsel for the defence. is one of the most searching cross-examiners at the English bar, but he made no impression on the astute chief of the Port Sunlight works. There was some delay before Counsel came into the Court on Wednesday. On entering Mr. Rufus Isaacs at once proceeded to explain that the fight was over, and added I withdraw unreservedly any imputation which has been made upon Mr. Lever's honour and integrity, and on behalf of my clients I say they much regret they have been misled into making the statements which have appeared in their paper, and which reflected so seriously upon him. At the earliest opportunity after hearing his evidence, and some of the statements he made which were quite new to as, we take the course which is the only. straightforward and honourable course for a newspaper to take under the circumstances. Realising what has happened, and that it has been mis- led, it takes the only means it can to repair the injury. I repeat that without any reservation of any sort we not only withdraw the imputations, but deeply regret they should have been made, and that we should have been misled into making them with regard to Mr. Lever."-

Mr. Rufus Isaacs, after a consultation with plaintiffs' counsel, announced that a settlement had been arrived at. The settlement, he said, was not only on the action against the "Daily Mail," but in the action which succeeded it in respect of libels by the "Daily Mirror." The observations he had made, and his unreserved witn- drawal and expressions of regret in reference to the libel in the first case, applied equally to the second. "Having taken the coarse which we thought right to take this morning," added counsel. "we have been anxious that we should meet the plaintiff company in no mean, niggardly, or grudging spirit as regards damages. They have formulated a claim on us, the amount of their damage in respect of both actions, at £50,000. Ido not desire,on behalf of the defendants to in any way reduce that sum. We agree to pal, the sum of £50,000 to them in settlement of the litigation,and as a matter of form my friend desires that a verdict should be taken by consent for this sum in the first action. The result of that will be there will be judgment for that amount with taxed costs in the first action in the second there must bo simple judgment for the costs, and that, I am glad to say, will pa& an end to this litigation." Sir Edward Carson said his friend had asked what sum Mr. Lever wished to name as the proper measure and mark of the damage, and Mr. Lever said £50,000. "My friend has accepted our own measure of damages, and there will be a verdict and judgment, therefore, for £50,000. I ask your lordship to enter judgment for the plaintiffs with costs for that amount.' His lordship expressed his satisfaction at the settle- ment, adding : " It would be very ungracious of me to express any criticism of the articles which formed the subject matter of the libel, after the course which has been taken • and, therefore, I do not propose to do so. I cap only say this, that if I had been called upon to deal with those articles, and there had been nothing more to justify them than has appeared in the course of cross- examination, as far as it has gone, I should have felt it my duty to express myself in no hesitt.t;ng or measured language as to the character of those articles. But since the parties have wisely agreed to the course we have just heard there is nothing further for you of for me to do than to tell you to return a verdict for £5OOOOO for the plaintiffs."

The jury did as requested, and judgment was give with costs in both actions.

ligE. COST OF THE CASE.

It is expeded that the costs on both side, including those of the solicitors and the preparation of the case and collection of evidence, will fall very little short of five figures—if at all.

RECORD DAMAGES. The damages of £50,000 are by far the largest ever paid in connection with a libel action in this country. The previous largest was £!2,000 awarded by the jury in the Kitson v. Playfair suit in 1896. The previous record in an action of any kind was £25,000 awarded in the Con- stantinidi divorce suit. FURTHER ACTIONS PROBABLE.

A Leeds correspondent says that in the course of an interview on Thursday night Messrs. Day and Yewdall, of the Central Bank Chambers, Leeds, solicitors to Messrs. Joseph Watson and Sons, Limited, of the Whitehall-road Soap Works, confirmed the statement made some time ago by Mr. Day that an action against the Associated Newspaper, Ltd., is pending. It is for libel, and is expected to be heard next October. A separate action has been entered against the" Leeds and Yorkshire Mercury." Sir Edward Carson has been retained for the plaintiffs as leading counsel in both cases.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., and the Soap Combine.

Commenting on the verdict, in Thursday's "Daily News," Mr. P. W. Wilson, M.P., who writes" Pictures in Parliament," said As for the hero of the occasion—Mr. Lever—l am not surprised that one who heard his evidence should have been impressed by his cool, passionless, unanswerable candour. The names of Mr. Lever and of Mr. Crosfield —also a member of this House --were pilloried by the Moderates last March, all over London, and on many occasions I have discussed with both these gentlemen the large problem :of industrial combination from the side of capital. Mr. Lever frequently displays that kind of fatalism which accepts what it regards as economic destiny- "I am "—he is fond of telling us—" not an employer, but, like the rest of you, the servant of the public, which is the only real employer of labour." He makes no pretence that the building of Port Sunlight was an act of benevolence or philanthropy. "A successful busi- ness," he explains, "must have physically efficient workpeople.' When, earlier in the year, he introduced his Old Age Pensions Bill he argued for social reform in the same even, imperturbable strain—pleading for universal income tax—graduated— so that each citizen may know precisely what his country is costing:him. Clear, accnrate, unwavering thought dominates the whole man; his tactics may be hard, but they are the precise opposite to those tricks which were alleged by the "Daily Mail." The perils underlying largo combinations are admitted by none more frankly than, let us say, by Mr. Crosfield. But in Mr. Lever's evidence we have, perhaps for the first time, a demonstration on oath of the way in which, when al, country enjoys Free Trade, the prices of raw materials—rising, falling—immediately react upon the market for the finished commodity. Where the ports are unencumbered by tariffs, variations in price are extraordinarily delicate and rapid. But a ring fence woald at once destroy the consumer's automatic ssfeguard. To denounce the "Soap Trust" while defending Protection has thus been a fatal non sequitur. One word more. I have not the pleasure of a usual place in the Press Gallery, but, in that important quarter of the House, Mr. Lever's victory is hailed with, unbounded satisfaction. The Harmswortis Press has become a hideous menace to the profession of journalism, as honourably pursued in this country, and it is now hoped that fairness and accuracy may cease to be drugs in the market.

===== 0002601_19070720_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Letter from Mr. Crosileld

The following letter from Mr. Crostield appeared in yesterday's" Daily News": Sir.—Your Parliamentary representative refers to my having admitted•the perils of gnat combinations. What I may not have made quite clear was that, without any combination at all, manufacturers might for a few years rush down prices, and make things very awkward even for thoroughly up-to-date small manufacturors,

entitled under normal circumstances to hold their owir, and make 3 fair profit. That kind of extreme policy familiar in America, has never hitherto been adopted in the soap /trade in England, Mid I knew that Mr. Lever • and others who supported his scheme last autumn had not the faintsst intention of running prices down in this fashion, or of adopting any such policy if the work- ing arrangement had been carried through. That was one of the reasons why, during the campaign of calumny indulged in by the Yellow Press lastautunn I defended in several letters to the Press the policy Mr. Lever had initiated.

Another reason was that I knew those who would have been responsible for the conduct of affairs in our working arrangement, alliance, or combine—whichever be the most correct terra to use—would not have tolerated any policy for the inflation of prices. Undue cutting of prices would, of course, have been avoided, but the allegation that were we going to squeeze the public and rush prices up was just as untrue as the statements, made with charming inconsistency by the same papers, that we were going to rush prices down. As a matter of fact, the keenness of American manu- facturers to find an outlet for their soap abroad would always be a healthy check on English manufacturers if they attempted to charge extravagant prices as com- pared with cost of materials and of manufacture. And this, of course, is a fact of the utmost significance to all Free Traders, and indeed all consumers alike. It is the most important of the facts that would have differentiated Mr. Lever's proposed Combine from a Trust in any sense in which that word is used in England.

The charge that we were simply attempting to start an American Trust wasjust one of the many pieces of false coin that have since been nailed to the counter. As stated,in evidence in Liverpool, we desired not to puts stop to a certain amount of legitimate advertising, which I rather think may always be necessary, but to save wasting large sums of money which, when prices of raw materials allow of it, are squandered in frenzied advertising. We should also have been able to reduce the cost of manufacture in the country as a whole by exchanging information, and making use all round of the results acquired by practical experience on the one hand, and scientific research on the other. These large savings must, in the long run, I submit, have been to the benefit of both producer and consumer. John Morley says in effect that we must be content in government with a second best. In a sense the same thing may be said of trade under whatever conditions it is carried on, but while recognising that, I have argued before, and maintain now, that Mr. Lever's scheme would have been in the long run to the benefit of all concerned. This great trial in Liverpool has shown how flagrantly many people were misled by the wholesale circulation of mis-statements, and competitors though we are in business, 1 rejoice in the victory he has won.— Yours etc., A. H. CROSFIELD. Hyde Park Hotel, London, July 18th.

REGISTERED OFFICE. MARKET GATE CHAMBERS, WARRINGTON. PROSPECTUS. THIS COMPANY has been formed for the purpose of purchasing or acquiring on lease suitable estates or plots of . land in, or on the immediate outskirts of the town of Warrington with the intention of establishing GARDEN SUBURBS in order to provide the inhabitants of the town and neighbourhood with convenient, healthy and attractive residences at moderate rentals. The conditions surrounding the houses and homes of the people are vital to the well- being of the nation, and the movement which is rapidly spreading over England for the establishment of GARDEN CITIES AND SUBURBS indicates a way by A bleb, through the voluntary association of all classes, much may be done on practical and business lines to solve a difficult problem. The desire of the promoters is to localize the methods which have been successful in other places with a view to improving 1 the conditions under which many of the working people of Warrington at present have to live. EACti ESTATE will be carefully selected with due regard to accessibility, appearance, health. and economy of build- ing, and laid out as a whole under the best available expert advice. By adopting comprehensive methods of develop- ment in all their estates the promoters confidently anticipate that they will be able to effect substantial economies and to avoid much of the waste which is incidental to individual building and, at the name time, to make it possible for each suburb to have its own characteristics, the buildings to be planned and arranged with reference to the beauty and convenience of the whole. The houses may be erected either by the Company, or by subsidiary Companies or Societies, or by both, or by private enterprise, the Company retalaing the freehold, where such estate is vested in it. the ground rents which will be created being available for the payment of the Company's working expenses and fixed dividends. Preliminary steps have already been taken with a view to the formation of co-operative Tenants' Societies to assist in the experiment on the lines which have proved so successful at Baling and in other places, and it is hoped that one or more of these will be registered very shortly. FUND A- MENTAL FEATURES of the scheme are that ample provision shall be made for open spaces for ornamental or recreative Purposes, that each house shall have a garden, that not more than a certain specified niamber of houses per acre shall be erected, and that all plans shall be subject to the approval of the Company. NEGOTIATIONS are well advanced for the purchase of two sites, one at Great Sankey and the other at Grappenhall. The former is situated within ten minutes' walk of the Cor- poration Tram Service. It contains about 20 acres of undulat- ing land, with good roads on two sides. The owners are the Governors of the Warnineton Grammar School, and the price agreed to be paid is £225 per acre (see plan enclosed). Mr. i Bennett. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. Long, Mr. Parr, Mr. f Roberts and Mr. Ryland's, six of the Directors, (are Governors i of the Grammar School, but they have no personal pecuniary 1 interest in the proposed sale. The Grappen/sall site, known as Morris Brook Farm, contains about 22 acres. The owner Is Mrs. Mary Anne Rostock, of langsbridga, Devonshire, and the price agreed to be paid is £2950. The land is close to Latchford Locks, on the Cheshire aide, and in immediate proximity to several large works. Both sites are moat suit- able for the Company's purposes, and, if they are acquired, the Directors intend to proceed as quickly as possible with the promotion of Garden Suburbs thereon. No promotion money is being or has has been paid. A copy of the Memorandum and Articles of Association can be inspected at the offices of the Solietors to the Company at any time during business hours. A copy of the Memorandum is printed at the end of this Prospectus, and forms part of it. The Articles rf Asscciation of the Company provide, as regards the qualification of the Directors, as follows : 'B4. Until otherwise determined by a General Meeting, the number of the Directors shall not be less than 6 nor more than lb. The qualification of a Director shall be the holding in his own name in the Company's books shares of the Company of the nominal 7alue of £lO on which all calls for the time being made have been paid. "86. The Directors shall be paid out of the funds of the Company by way of remuneration for their services such sum as the Company in General Meeting may from time to time determine and such sum shall be divided among the Directors in such proportion and manner as the Directors may determine." It may be added that the Directors have already promised to take up over £3,000 in shares. The minimum subset's tion upon which the Directors will proceed to allotment has been fixed at £5,000. The preli- minary expenses of and incident to the formation and float- ing of the Company down to the time when it becomes entitled to commence business will be paid by the Company, and are estimated at £1.50. Application for shares should be made upon the accompany- ' ing farm, and sent to the Bankers of the Company. The Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Company, Limited, Sankey-street, Warrington; together with remit- tances for the amount payable on applications. Prospectuses and forms of application can be obtained at the Registered Offiats of the Company, or from the Solicitors i or Bankers. Dated 13th, July, 1907. THE RETURN ON THE SHARES will be limited to a cumulative dividend of Five Pounds per cent. per annum. All surplus profits,after Meeting the expenses of management and development, subject to the recommendations of the Directors, will be applied either in the improvement of the Company's properties or in the extension of the scheme in other directions. None of the elates of this issue are being or have been issued otherwise than for cash. No commission has been paid or is payable for the sub- scription or underwriting of any Shares of this issue.

presently she heard him pull up and sIIN hall dismount and examine his horse's shoes. "What is the matter, Brown ? " she called back. I "Cast a shoe, miss," he said. "Must a-done it getting over the rail." She stopped and hesitated. Lord Lynborough disliked her riding without a groom; but the ii)prnine was so fine, and she wanted a gallop so badly, that she was disinclined to go back. "You may go home," she said. "Tell his lordship, if you see him, that I shall not go far." The man touched his hat and led his horse seday, and Veronica, rather pleased to be alone, ride on. She left the park presently by one of the lodge gates, and, calming the mare into a trot, went down the high rc.ad towards Halsesaa the market town. Lynne Court is in the centre of the part ot Devonshire which has not yet been blessed—or cursed—by a railway. The nearest station is ten miles distant, and, in consequence, all the district is remote, and, if not melancholy, slow. It is a beautiful country,and as varied as beautiful. At one mile one may easily imagine oneself in the loveliest part of Surrey, at the next the wide- stretching moorland is absurdly Scottish, and but a mile or two further it is possible to come upon a valley which is Switzerland on a small saale. Veronica had grown to love every inch of it. And now she was going to be mistress of a part of it; for the dower house and estate of Wayne- ford were but fifteen miles from Lynne Court. "To-morrow go over and look at it," she said to herself. "How sordid and selfish that sounds. 'Riches corrupt most minds.' I remember that copy-book heading. Am I already corrupted, I wonder?, Am I different to the girl who stood not so very long ago before the great Earl, shaking with nervousness and yet quivering with the proud determination not to shew it ? " She looked backwards to the eventful morning of her arrival at the Court, and saw herself as she was then, thin and pale, in her poor, cheap, plain black dress, and then recalled the face and figure which the pier-glass had reflected this morning as Goodwin put on the costly, perfectly- fitting habit. I "Yes, lam different," she thought. "How could I help being so ? " After awhile she left the road and struck across the moor, and giving her mare its head, raced over the short, springy heather. Suddenly she I saw two small objects moving in front of her. At first she thought they were rabbits, then she saw that they were a fox-terrier and a puppy. Mamma was sitting on her haunches proudly watching the antics of her offspring, and when she heard the mare she looked up and gave a short yap of warning to the puppy. "All right, doggie " said Veronica, smiling; • and she was drawing the mare out of the way when the horse startdd and shied at something on the off side, and was nearly upon the puppy. The neat moment a man rose from where he had I been lying, and with a movement of amazing rapidity rushed under the horse's legs, as it seemed to Veronica, and caught the wee mite up out of its peril. llt was so neatly, so audaciously, done, that Veronica's breath came sharply, and the colour rose to her face. "Oh, thank you," she said, quickly. "Is it hurt ? " "Not in the least," he said. "Down, old girl !" to the mother, who leaped up at her puppy, yapping imploringly and anxiously. "I am glad, ' said Veronica. "I was afraid I had ridden over it. Indeed, I should have done so if you had not snatched it up in time. Let me have it, please." ' He handed her the soft little mite, and, of course, she pressed it to her cheek ; then she gave it back to him. "It was my fault," he Laid, holding the puppy , to him and cosseting it, evidently with the approval of the mother, who had thrown herself I down at his feet and was gazing up at puppy and I man with complacent pride. 'I was asleep on the heather, and your horse shied At me. I'm sorry, and hope you'dl forgive me." Veronica inclined her head. She was trying to keep her surprise from revealing itself in her too I expressive eyes, for though the ,man's voice, ' tone, and bearing were those of a 6ntleman, he was dressed like a labouring man—a gamekeeper, was her first thought. He was strikingly good- looking, with short, etasp brown hair, and lashes ! almost too long for a man. But they were the only touch of femininity about him, for the rest Of the face was masculine enough' and his form was eloquent of strength and the ease that aponmnanies it. "Well, tnere Is no harm done," sheesaid. "I suppose the doggie and its bairn belong to the cottage over there." "Very probably," he said. over and see." Heiraised his hat—she noticed that_ he raised it insrktd of touching it—and was moving away with the puppy still in his hand, when he hesitated and said: "I beg your pardon, but can you tell me the way to Lynne Court ? " "Yes," replied Veronica. "If you follow the road, that way "—she pointed with her whip— "you will come to the South lodge. I have just come from the Court." "Perhaps you'd know, then— Oh, but of course you wouldn't—it's absurd of me," he broke off with a smile—; smile' which lit up a I Incidents in the careers of well-known people are of face that had vaguely struck Veronica as rather special interest to the public ; recognising this a representative waited upon Miss Amy Corallie at the a sad one. WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY. , -- Resignation of The Manager. .---- The quarterly meeting of the Warrington Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society; Ltd. was held on Monday night in the Co-operative Half, Sankey-street. Mr. T. Critchley (president) occupied the chair, and was supported by the following members of the com- mittee :-Messrs. T. Wadsworth, B. Hall, F. Timperley, B. Farrar' W. Bibby, C. H. Hibbert, E. Malone, N. Cook, W. Haigh, G. T. Rushton, D. Donellon, and T. Bowers. The room was crowded with members. The PRESIDENT said• he Lad no hesitation in saying' that the financial position of the Society was second to none in the country. In the near future large exten- sions and alterations were contemplated, and it behoved them to be extremely careful how they dealt with with their reserve. They proposed among other things to erect a central warehouse which might cost from £19,000 to £15,000. Their late worthy manager and secretary (Mr. A. H. Rathbone) was not with them that night and would not be with them again. The question of his resignation was adjourned from the last quarterly Meeting to that one for Mr. Rathbone to con- sider what he should do. He had decided what course to take, and on July 3rd he placed before the committee his final resignation with the desire that his services should terminate on Saturday;' the 13th. Since receiv- ing the resignation, the committee had met and decided to advertise for a manager and e secretary. Mr. Rath- bone's decision not to take any further active part in the management of the society was the outcome of his being unable to fall in with certain views and actions taken during the past two years. He (the President) bad very great respect for Mr.Rathbone, and considered they could not have had a better man to control affairs. He had been an excellent chief, and conscientiously endeavoured to build up a sound financial undertaking. Great credit was due to him- for the sound condition in which he left the Society. He (the President) believed that if Mr. Rathbone had not been such an upright and conscientious man he could have benefited hmiself very much financially without impoverishing the society. The greatest fault they could find with -•-• him was that he had saved too much money for the If you are thin, wet'; or deallitated from any cause benefit of the members. They could not speak too or if you suffer from indigestion, nervousness, or highly of him now he had left them and he (the palpitation, a certain ye t perfectly harmless and invalu- President) hoped Mr. Rathbone would' live long to enjoy the retirement he had so well earned. taken in pledge. At one time it was feared that adjoining shops would be endangered, but the firemen were successful in confining the flames to Mr. Tyrer's premises. A splendid supply of water was obtained from the street hydrants and in about an hour after the outbreak was discovered the fire was got under control. Exciting scenes were witnessed the brigade having a difficult task in removing a large quantity of goods, many articles having to be lowered through the windows. A large crowd assembled, but the police arrangements were of such an excellent character that the work of the firemen was not impeded. The damage is estimated at 21,000.
